## Genesis 12:10 (id: 12290)

The Nile River supplied plenty of water for crops, so Egypt was usually the last area to experience famine.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 12:10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 12:10–13 (id: 12288)

Abram’s scheme was rooted in fear that jeopardized his family and God’s promises. Abram was not walking by faith when he went to Egypt. He stopped building altars and his deceptiveness took center stage. Deception would plague his family throughout Genesis ([26:1–11](https://ref.ly/Gen26:1-Gen26:11); [27:1–29](https://ref.ly/Gen27:1-Gen27:29); [29:15–30](https://ref.ly/Gen29:15-Gen29:30); [30:34–36](https://ref.ly/Gen30:34-Gen30:36); [31:6–11](https://ref.ly/Gen31:6-Gen31:11); [37:18–35](https://ref.ly/Gen37:18-Gen37:35); [39:7–20](https://ref.ly/Gen39:7-Gen39:20)). • Abram’s plan was probably based on a social custom whereby a brother arranged the marriage of his sister (cp. [24:29–61](https://ref.ly/Gen24:29-Gen24:61)). Abram may have thought that any potential suitor would have to deal with him, giving him time to leave with Sarai. He did not count on Pharaoh’s acting without negotiation ([12:14–16](https://ref.ly/Gen12:14-Gen12:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 12:10-13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`)

## Genesis 12:10–20 (id: 12289)

This episode shows that God would not allow Abram to jeopardize his promises. Just after Abram’s obedience to the call, a famine tested his weak faith. God delivered him and his family, even though Abram foolishly used deception rather than trusting in God to preserve him in Egypt. • This story deliberately parallels Israel’s later bondage in Egypt. Because of a famine ([Gen 12:10](https://ref.ly/Gen12:10) *//* [Gen 47:13](https://ref.ly/Gen47:13)), Abram/Israel went to Egypt ([Gen 12:10](https://ref.ly/Gen12:10) *//* [Gen 47:27](https://ref.ly/Gen47:27)); there was an attempt to kill the males and save the females ([Gen 12:12](https://ref.ly/Gen12:12) *//* [Exod 1:22](https://ref.ly/Exod1:22)); God plagued Egypt ([Gen 12:17](https://ref.ly/Gen12:17) *//* [Exod 7:14–11:10](https://ref.ly/Exod7:14-Exod11:10)); Abram/Israel plundered Egypt ([Gen 12:16](https://ref.ly/Gen12:16) *//* [Exod 12:35–36](https://ref.ly/Exod12:35-Exod12:36)); they were expelled (Hebrew shalakh, “send”; [Gen 12:19–20](https://ref.ly/Gen12:19-Gen12:20) *//* [Exod 12:31–33](https://ref.ly/Exod12:31-Exod12:33)) and ascended to the Negev ([Gen 13:1](https://ref.ly/Gen13:1) *//* [Num 13:17](https://ref.ly/Num13:17), [22](https://ref.ly/Num13:22)). Israel was to believe that God would deliver them from bondage in Egypt through the plagues because their ancestor had already been rescued from bondage in Egypt.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 12:10-20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.3`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Camel (ID: `fauna:Camel`)

## Genesis 12:13 (id: 12291)

tell them you are my sister: This request occurs three times in Genesis (see also [20:2](https://ref.ly/Gen20:2); [26:7](https://ref.ly/Gen26:7)). The text explains that this was Abram’s usual strategy ([20:13](https://ref.ly/Gen20:13)), and his son did likewise. This first occasion was outside the land, the second (ch [20](https://ref.ly/Gen20:1-Gen20:18)) within, showing that God protected his promise in both regions.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 12:13


## Genesis 12:14–15 (id: 12292)

Sarai was 65 years old, but she lived to be 127; she was like a modern childless woman of about 35\. She and Abram came from a noble family (see study note on 11:29), so she was regal in her person and dress. Pharaoh was attracted by her physical appearance and her political assets.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 12:14-15
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 11:29 (ID: `12275@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.3`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`)

## Genesis 12:14–16 (id: 12293)

Abram was bound by the king’s gift to an unwanted agreement about Sarai that he could not prevent. His scheme had resulted in a terrible bind that endangered him, Sarai, and the promise. • Abram appeared to prosper from his deception, but the new possessions also caused crises. Abram and Lot had to separate (ch [13](https://ref.ly/Gen13:1-Gen13:18)), and Hagar, an Egyptian maiden, became the mother of the Ishmaelites, perennial enemies of Israel (ch [16](https://ref.ly/Gen16:1-Gen16:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 12:14-16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.3`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Camel (ID: `fauna:Camel`)

## Genesis 12:15 (id: 12294)

Pharaoh was a title, not a personal name ([37:36](https://ref.ly/Gen37:36); [Exod 1:15](https://ref.ly/Exod1:15)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 12:15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.3`)

## Genesis 12:17–19 (id: 12295)

God’s intervention rescued Sarai and preserved the marriage to fulfill the covenant promise. Sarai’s restoration to Abram came with a rebuke from Pharaoh on God’s behalf ([12:18–19](https://ref.ly/Gen12:18-Gen12:19)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 12:17-19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.3`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`)

## Genesis 12:20 (id: 12297)

No answer to Pharaoh’s questions ([12:18–19](https://ref.ly/Gen12:18-Gen12:19)) was needed, because the rebuke was followed by expulsion. Pharaoh’s command paralleled God’s command to Abram ([12:1](https://ref.ly/Gen12:1)), but Pharaoh’s demand brought shame and disgrace. God was faithful in preserving his promise.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 12:20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.3`)

## Genesis 13:1–7 (id: 12307)

This story is set in conflict amid God’s blessings. In the opening verses, Abram returns to a place where he had built an altar. Previous events are emphasized as Abram’s return to the land is described ([13:3–4](https://ref.ly/Gen13:3-Gen13:4)); Abram renewed his worship and again proclaimed the Lord’s name (cp. [12:8](https://ref.ly/Gen12:8)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 13:1-7

## Genesis 13:2 (id: 12314)

Abram already had powerful resources ([12:5](https://ref.ly/Gen12:5)). His time in Egypt increased his wealth and power ([12:16](https://ref.ly/Gen12:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 13:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Genesis 13:5–7 (id: 12315)

Lot was also wealthy, with flocks and herds. Tents figure prominently in Lot’s story ([13:12](https://ref.ly/Gen13:12)). • The Canaanites and Perizzites (see [34:30](https://ref.ly/Gen34:30); [Deut 7:1](https://ref.ly/Deut7:1); [Judg 1:4](https://ref.ly/Judg1:4); [3:5](https://ref.ly/Judg3:5)) held the well\-watered land; the quarrel between Abram’s and Lot’s herdsmen left Abram more vulnerable to attack.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 13:5-7

## Genesis 13:8 (id: 12317)

Abram was concerned that there be no conflict (Hebrew meribah) between them, as they were close relatives (literally *brothers*). Moses later reproved Israel over the incident in the wilderness at Meribah ([Exod 17:1–7](https://ref.ly/Exod17:1-Exod17:7); [Num 20:1–13](https://ref.ly/Num20:1-Num20:13)) and instructed them on exercising faith in such situations. Meribah thereafter became a watchword for testing and striving with the Lord in unbelief (see [Ps 95](https://ref.ly/Ps95:1-Ps95:11)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 13:8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lot (ID: `person:Lot`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Genesis 13:8–13 (id: 12316)

Abram, to whom the land was promised, might have told Lot to find his own place. Abram’s generosity was an act of faith; he knew that even if he gave the whole land away, God would still give it to him and his descendants. Abram did not have to cling to things, whereas Lot’s choices were self\-seeking.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 13:8-13

## Genesis 13:10 (id: 12308)

What appealed to Lot would be short\-lived. In the garden of the Lord, Adam and Eve succumbed to their craving for what they saw; Israel was later enslaved in Egypt. Sodom and Gomorrah are reminders of putting intense desires ahead of obedience to God ([3:5–6](https://ref.ly/Gen3:5-Gen3:6); see [1 Jn 2:16](https://ref.ly/1John2:16)). • Zoar was a small town in the plain to which Lot and his daughters later fled ([19:18–22](https://ref.ly/Gen19:18-Gen19:22)); it was previously called Bela ([14:2](https://ref.ly/Gen14:2)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 13:10

Lot’s choice was totally selfish, without concern for Abram or faith in the Lord. • The region called the whole Jordan Valley (literally *the circle of the Jordan*) is believed to have been near the south end of the Dead Sea, based on descriptions in ancient records that locate cities of the Plain. This area is now very desolate.

## Genesis 13:18 (id: 12313)

Hebron was an Anakite city ([Num 13:22](https://ref.ly/Num13:22)) originally called Kiriath\-arba (“city of Arba”), located in forested highlands just north of the Negev ([Gen 12:9](https://ref.ly/Gen12:9); [Josh 17:15](https://ref.ly/Josh17:15)). Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob all settled there ([Gen 18:1](https://ref.ly/Gen18:1); [35:27](https://ref.ly/Gen35:27); [37:14](https://ref.ly/Gen37:14)), and Sarah, Abraham, Isaac, Rebekah, Jacob, and Leah were buried there ([23:19](https://ref.ly/Gen23:19); [35:27–29](https://ref.ly/Gen35:27-Gen35:29); [49:29–32](https://ref.ly/Gen49:29-Gen49:32); [50:13](https://ref.ly/Gen50:13)). • Mamre was an Amorite ([14:13](https://ref.ly/Gen14:13); [15:16](https://ref.ly/Gen15:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 13:18

## Genesis 14:4 (id: 12331)

King Kedorlaomer apparently defeated the Siddim Valley kings at an earlier time and put them under tribute for asphalt, olive oil, and copper. In the thirteenth year they refused to send it; in the fourteenth year ([14:5](https://ref.ly/Gen14:5)), the invaders returned to subjugate them again.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 14:4

## Genesis 14:4–5 (id: 12330)

This was Chedorlaomer’s war. Under the feudal system of tribal affiliations, those in covenant with him had to fight. It was also Abram’s battle to rescue Lot, and those under treaty with him had to accompany him.

The word Hebrew first occurs here in the Bible. It is not equivalent to the later term *Habiru* from Egyptian texts; the *Habiru* were mercenaries that roamed the land in the era of the judges. • Mamre: See study note on 13:18. • relatives: Or *allies;* literally *brothers.*

## Genesis 14:14 (id: 12322)

Abram . . . mobilized the 318 trained men who had been born into his household: Abram was a formidable force, an outworking of God’s promise to make him great ([12:2–3](https://ref.ly/Gen12:2-Gen12:3)). • Dan was a city about 150 miles north of Abram’s home in Hebron, then named Laish or Leshem (see [Josh 19:47](https://ref.ly/Josh19:47) and study note; [Judg 18:29](https://ref.ly/Judg18:29)). Dan, whose descendants migrated north in the days of the judges ([Judg 18:1–29](https://ref.ly/Judg18:1-Judg18:29)), had not yet been born ([Gen 30:6](https://ref.ly/Gen30:6)). An editor apparently updated the text so that later readers could identify this city.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 14:14
* **Associated Articles:** Joshua 19:47 (ID: `19454@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Dan (ID: `place:Dan`)

## Genesis 14:14–16 (id: 12321)

God could give his people victory over any forces invading the Promised Land. Faithfulness to God was the prerequisite for victory. God promises to bless his people and give them victory over the world. He uses those who respond to his call and can skillfully use weapons of war (cp. [Eph 4:8](https://ref.ly/Eph4:8); [6:10–19](https://ref.ly/Eph6:10-Eph6:19)).

[Ps 76:2](https://ref.ly/Ps76:2)).

## Genesis 15:1 (id: 12342)

Do not be afraid: Abram lacked a son to be his heir. The Lord addressed Abram’s anxiety about the future with comforting words. • I will protect you (literally *I will be your shield*): The Hebrew word for “shield” (magen) is from the same root as Melchizedek’s word *defeated* ([14:20](https://ref.ly/Gen14:20)). The Lord who had defeated Abram’s enemies would continue to protect him. • your reward will be great: The promise of offspring ([12:2–3](https://ref.ly/Gen12:2-Gen12:3); cp. [Ps 127:3](https://ref.ly/Ps127:3)) was still unfulfilled.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 15:1

## Genesis 15:1–21 (id: 12334)

God made a covenant (a special agreement) with Abram. In this covenant, God confirmed the promises he had made earlier when he summoned Abram to leave his country ([12:1–3](https://ref.ly/Gen12:1-Gen12:3)). Abram's descendants would experience a long period of slavery before God made these promises come true.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 15:1-21

## Genesis 15:2–3 (id: 12343)

Using a wordplay, Abram expressed his concern that Eliezer of Damascus (Hebrew dammeseq), a man in Abram’s household, would be his heir (Hebrew ben\-mesheq*,* “son of possession”), as was customary when there was no son.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 15:2-3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Eliezer (ID: `person:Eliezer.2`); Damascus (ID: `place:Damascus`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Dispossess (ID: `keyterm:Dispossess`)

## Genesis 15:4–6 (id: 12344)

God affirmed that the promise was for Abram’s own offspring and showed him the stars as a promise of the vast number of descendants that he would have ([22:17](https://ref.ly/Gen22:17); [26:4](https://ref.ly/Gen26:4)). Paul quotes this promise in [Rom 4:18](https://ref.ly/Rom4:18) to underscore the strength of Abram’s faith.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 15:4-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Believe In (ID: `keyterm:BelieveIn`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Dispossess (ID: `keyterm:Dispossess`)

## Genesis 15:6 (id: 12345)

And Abram believed: God made his covenant with a believer; the statement does not indicate when Abram came to faith. The Hebrew text does not link Abram’s belief with the promise of the stars; it just says parenthetically that Abram believed God. Abram already had faith; his departure from Ur was his first great act that demonstrated it (see [Heb 11:8–10](https://ref.ly/Heb11:8-Heb11:10)). • God counted him as righteous because of his faith: This central statement about Abram’s saving faith is quoted three times in the New Testament ([Rom 4:3](https://ref.ly/Rom4:3); [Gal 3:6](https://ref.ly/Gal3:6); [Jas 2:23](https://ref.ly/Jas2:23)) to support the doctrine of righteousness before God by faith (see also [Rom 4:22–23](https://ref.ly/Rom4:22-Rom4:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 15:6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Believe In (ID: `keyterm:BelieveIn`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 15:7–21 (id: 12346)

In a special ceremony, God made a covenant with Abram and promised to do what he had said.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 15:7-21

## Genesis 15:10 (id: 12335)

Obeying God’s instructions, Abram gathered three herd animals for the ceremony and cut them in half. Cutting the animals symbolized the oath, indicating that the covenant maker staked his own life on his word ([Jer 34:18](https://ref.ly/Jer34:18)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 15:10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bird (ID: `fauna:Bird`)

## Genesis 15:11 (id: 12336)

*birds of prey:* These are ceremonially unclean birds that symbolize those who unjustly attack Abraham's heirs ([15:13–14](https://ref.ly/Gen15:13-Gen15:14)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 15:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Vulture (ID: `fauna:Vulture`)

## Genesis 15:13 (id: 12338)

oppressed: The same word is used in [Exod 1:11–12](https://ref.ly/Exod1:11-Exod1:12). Egypt, like predatory birds ([Gen 15:11](https://ref.ly/Gen15:11)), would try to destroy Israel and hinder the covenant’s fulfillment. • Apparently 400 years is a round number (also [Acts 7:6](https://ref.ly/Acts7:6); cp. [Exod 12:40](https://ref.ly/Exod12:40); [Gal 3:17](https://ref.ly/Gal3:17)). Using the chronology in the Hebrew text, the family moved to Egypt around 1876 BC, and the Exodus occurred around 1446 BC (though many scholars date the Exodus later, around 1270 BC; see Exodus Book Introduction, “The Date of the Exodus”).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 15:13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 15:13–16 (id: 12337)

Not even 400 years of slavery could interfere with God’s plan to fulfill the covenant.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 15:13-16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Amorites (ID: `group:Amorites`); Peace (ID: `keyterm:IntactState`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 15:16 (id: 12339)

The reasons for Israel’s bondage included God’s justice. God would tolerate the sins of the Amorites until they fully deserved judgment. • do not yet warrant their destruction (literally *are not yet full*): To give the Promised Land to Israel, the Lord would dispossess the land’s inhabitants in a way that satisfied his justice. The fulfillment of promises to Israel also brought retributive judgment on people of the land (though individuals were saved by faith; see [Josh 2:1–15](https://ref.ly/Josh2:1-Josh2:15); [6:23–25](https://ref.ly/Josh6:23-Josh6:25); [Heb 11:31](https://ref.ly/Heb11:31); [Jas 2:25](https://ref.ly/Jas2:25)). Until then, God would send the family to Egypt where Israel could become a great nation. Seeing all this in advance was terrifying ([Gen 15:12](https://ref.ly/Gen15:12)), but it was comforting to know that nothing could interfere with God’s plan.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 15:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Amorites (ID: `group:Amorites`)

## Genesis 15:17–18 (id: 12340)

smoking firepot . . . flaming torch: Fire represented the Lord’s cleansing, consuming zeal and unapproachable holiness (cp. [Isa 6:3–7](https://ref.ly/Isa6:3-Isa6:7)). The holy God made (literally *cut*) a unilateral covenant with Abram; its promises were absolutely sure because they did not depend on what Abram or his descendants might do.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 15:17-18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Euphrates River (ID: `place:Euphrates`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 15:18–19 (id: 12341)

God specified the boundaries of the Promised Land. His clear message to Abram was that despite prospects of death and suffering (enslavement), he and his descendants would eventually receive the promises, for God had sworn an oath (see [Heb 6:13–14](https://ref.ly/Heb6:13-Heb6:14)). Nothing can separate God’s people from his love or the fulfillment of his plans (see [Rom 8:18–39](https://ref.ly/Rom8:18-Rom8:39); [2 Pet 1:3–4](https://ref.ly/2Pet1:3-2Pet1:4)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 15:18-19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Euphrates River (ID: `place:Euphrates`); Kenizzites (ID: `group:Kenizzites`); Kadmonites (ID: `group:Kadmonites`); Kenites (ID: `group:Kenite`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 16:1–3 (id: 12348)

Abram and Sarai faced the tension of her being barren and beyond childbearing years. By custom, a barren woman could give her servant to her husband as a slave\-wife; the child born to that union was considered the wife’s child and could be adopted as the heir. Sarai’s suggestion, unobjectionable by custom, set a problematic human plan in motion. God’s promises would be fulfilled by faith.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 16:1-3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Hagar (ID: `person:Hagar`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`)

## Genesis 16:1–16 (id: 12347)

While they waited for their promised son to be born, Abram and Sarai tried another plan. This plan did not show trust in God.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 16:1-16

Hagar might have thought Abram would favor her instead of Sarai (compare [Proverbs 30:21–23](https://ref.ly/Prov30:21-Prov30:23)).

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Hagar (ID: `person:Hagar`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 16:7 (id: 12354)

The angel of the Lord was the Lord himself ([16:13](https://ref.ly/Gen16:13); [21:17](https://ref.ly/Gen21:17); [22:11–12](https://ref.ly/Gen22:11-Gen22:12); [31:11–13](https://ref.ly/Gen31:11-Gen31:13); [48:16](https://ref.ly/Gen48:16); [Exod 3:2](https://ref.ly/Exod3:2); [32:34](https://ref.ly/Exod32:34); [Judg 6:11](https://ref.ly/Judg6:11), [16](https://ref.ly/Judg6:16), [22](https://ref.ly/Judg6:22); [13:22–23](https://ref.ly/Judg13:22-Judg13:23); [Zech 3:1–2](https://ref.ly/Zech3:1-Zech3:2)) but was also distinct from the Lord ([Gen 24:7](https://ref.ly/Gen24:7); [2 Sam 24:16](https://ref.ly/2Sam24:16); [Zech 1:12](https://ref.ly/Zech1:12)). The angel of the Lord was probably a *theophany* (a manifestation of God) or a *Christophany* (an appearance of the pre\-incarnate Messiah; see [Gen 18:1–2](https://ref.ly/Gen18:1-Gen18:2); [19:1](https://ref.ly/Gen19:1); [Num 22:22](https://ref.ly/Num22:22); [Judg 2:1–4](https://ref.ly/Judg2:1-Judg2:4); [5:23](https://ref.ly/Judg5:23); [Zech 12:8](https://ref.ly/Zech12:8)), speaking with the authority of the Lord himself.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 16:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Shur (ID: `place:Shur`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`)

## Genesis 16:8–12 (id: 12355)

The angel’s rhetorical questions encouraged Hagar to pour out her heart to God. When she did, God commanded her to return and submit ([16:9](https://ref.ly/Gen16:9)), promising that her son would have innumerable descendants. The angel of the Lord never referred to Hagar as Abram’s wife, only as Sarai’s servant. She would have Abram’s child, but Ishmael was not central to God’s covenant with Abram.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 16:8-12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); Hagar (ID: `person:Hagar`); Ishmael (ID: `person:Ishmael`); Wild Donkey (ID: `fauna:WildDonkey`)

## Genesis 16:10–12 (id: 12349)

Hagar’s son would become the father of a great but wild and hostile nation living in the Arabian Desert as perennial enemies of Israel (cp. [25:18](https://ref.ly/Gen25:18)). God blessed Ishmael as Abram’s descendant, but not as the line chosen to carry on the covenant. That blessing was reserved for Abram’s chosen heir.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 16:10-12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Ishmael (ID: `person:Ishmael`); Wild Donkey (ID: `fauna:WildDonkey`)

## Genesis 16:11 (id: 12350)

Names in Genesis often capture the message of a passage and aid the remembrance of the events and their significance in the history of the faith. The name Ishmael, which means “God hears,” commemorates that the Lord . . . heard Hagar’s cry of distress (see also study note on 16:14–15). This name would have greatly comforted Hagar; God listened to her prayers and acknowledged her complaint.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 16:11
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 16:14-15 (ID: `12352@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Ishmael (ID: `person:Ishmael`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`)

## Genesis 16:13 (id: 12351)

Hagar responded to God’s messages by faith, in her words and in her obedience. • the God who sees me: God knew Hagar’s plight and watched over her.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 16:13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 16:14–15 (id: 12352)

The names Beer\-lahai\-roi, which means “well of the Living One who sees me,” and Ishmael (see [16:11](https://ref.ly/Gen16:11)) were a message and a rebuke for Abram and Sarai. God sees affliction and hears the cries of those in need. Sarai and Abram should have prayed rather than taking the fulfillment of the promise into their own hands by following social custom (cp. [25:21](https://ref.ly/Gen25:21)). Giving children to the barren woman is God’s work ([Ps 113:9](https://ref.ly/Ps113:9); cp. [1 Sam 1:1–28](https://ref.ly/1Sam1:1-1Sam1:28); [Luke 1:1–25](https://ref.ly/Luke1:1-Luke1:25)); impossible difficulties cannot be resolved by human intervention. The Lord hears the afflicted, sees them in their need, and will miraculously provide for them.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 16:14-15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bered (ID: `place:Bered`); Hagar (ID: `person:Hagar`); Beer-lahai-roi (ID: `place:Beer-lahai-roi`); Ishmael (ID: `person:Ishmael`); Kadesh-barnea (ID: `place:Kadesh-barnea`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 17:1 (id: 12361)

El\-Shaddai: This name for God emphasizes his power (see also [28:3](https://ref.ly/Gen28:3); [35:11](https://ref.ly/Gen35:11); [43:14](https://ref.ly/Gen43:14); [48:3](https://ref.ly/Gen48:3); [49:25](https://ref.ly/Gen49:25)). • Serve me faithfully and live a blameless life: Being a blessing to the nations required obedience from Abram; his conduct would be guided by Almighty God.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 17:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Complete (ID: `keyterm:Complete`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 17:1–27 (id: 12356)

God now gave the family signs that the promises would be fulfilled. He changed Abram’s name to Abraham ([17:1–8](https://ref.ly/Gen17:1-Gen17:8)), instituted the rite of circumcision as the sign of the covenant ([17:9–14](https://ref.ly/Gen17:9-Gen17:14), [23–27](https://ref.ly/Gen17:23-Gen17:27)), and changed Sarai’s name to Sarah ([17:15–22](https://ref.ly/Gen17:15-Gen17:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 17:1-27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Circumcision (ID: `keyterm:Circumcision`); Foreskin (ID: `keyterm:Foreskin`); Ishmael (ID: `person:Ishmael`); Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Uncircumcised Person (ID: `keyterm:UncircumcisedPerson`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Complete (ID: `keyterm:Complete`)

## Genesis 17:4–5 (id: 12364)

God guaranteed his promise by changing Abram’s name. Abram referred to his noble lineage, as Terah was the “exalted father” ([11:27](https://ref.ly/Gen11:27)). His new name, Abraham (Hebrew ’ab hamon) was a wordplay on the promise of his own progeny (see also [John 8:31–59](https://ref.ly/John8:31-John8:59); [Rom 4:16–17](https://ref.ly/Rom4:16-Rom4:17); [Gal 3:7](https://ref.ly/Gal3:7), [15–19](https://ref.ly/Gal3:15-Gal3:19), [29](https://ref.ly/Gal3:29)). Whenever the new name was used, he and his household would remember that a multitude of nations would issue from him.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 17:4-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`)

## Genesis 17:6 (id: 12365)

*kings will descend from you:* This is the first sign that Israel would become a nation ruled by kings (see also [35:11](https://ref.ly/Gen35:11); [36:31](https://ref.ly/Gen36:31); [Numbers 24:7](https://ref.ly/Num24:7); [Deuteronomy 17:14–18](https://ref.ly/Deut17:14-Deut17:18); [28:36](https://ref.ly/Deut28:36)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 17:6

God gave circumcision as a confirming sign that reminded all households of loyalty to the covenant.

## Genesis 17:14 (id: 12357)

will be cut off: This punishment seems to have several applications. A person could be exiled from society or put to death by the community; most often it warned that a person might die prematurely as God cut him off from the land of the living (see [Exod 31:14](https://ref.ly/Exod31:14); [Lev 7:20–27](https://ref.ly/Lev7:20-Lev7:27); [17:3–4](https://ref.ly/Lev17:3-Lev17:4); [20:17–18](https://ref.ly/Lev20:17-Lev20:18); [23:28–29](https://ref.ly/Lev23:28-Lev23:29); [Num 15:30–31](https://ref.ly/Num15:30-Num15:31); see also [Ps 31:22](https://ref.ly/Ps31:22); [Ezek 21:4](https://ref.ly/Ezek21:4); [Rom 9:3](https://ref.ly/Rom9:3); [11:22](https://ref.ly/Rom11:22)). Failure to be circumcised was a serious violation (see [Exod 4:24–26](https://ref.ly/Exod4:24-Exod4:26); cp. [Gal 5:2–4](https://ref.ly/Gal5:2-Gal5:4)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 17:14

Sarah: The new name, fitting for one who would be the mother of kings, was a milestone in Sarah’s calling and brought attention to the promise.

The name Isaac (Hebrew yitskhaq, “he laughs”) would constantly recall Abraham’s disbelieving laughter when he heard the promise. It was also a reminder of God’s favor and his pleasure in the birth (cp. [21:6](https://ref.ly/Gen21:6)).

Having received God’s word about Isaac, Abraham immediately complied with God’s instructions. He implemented the rite of circumcision as an act of faith; it signified their participation in the covenant (cp. [Rom 4:11–12](https://ref.ly/Rom4:11-Rom4:12); [Gal 5:2–6](https://ref.ly/Gal5:2-Gal5:6), [11](https://ref.ly/Gal5:11); [6:15](https://ref.ly/Gal6:15); [Phil 3:2–3](https://ref.ly/Phil3:2-Phil3:3); [Col 2:11–12](https://ref.ly/Col2:11-Col2:12); [1 Pet 3:21](https://ref.ly/1Pet3:21)).

## Genesis 18:1–15 (id: 12368)

The Lord’s visit to Abraham set the time for Isaac’s birth. The three visitors were probably the Lord and two angels (see study note on 16:7). Abraham’s peaceful and generous reception of the visitors contrasts sharply with the chaos and corruption of Sodom (ch [19](https://ref.ly/Gen19:1-Gen19:38)). Eating together was important in making or confirming covenants; when God was ready to fulfill the covenant promise, he came in person to share a meal with Abraham. Fellowship with God has always been signified by a communal meal (see [Exod 24:9–11](https://ref.ly/Exod24:9-Exod24:11); [Matt 26:17–30](https://ref.ly/Matt26:17-Matt26:30) *//* [Luke 22:7–38](https://ref.ly/Luke22:7-Luke22:38); [Acts 2:42](https://ref.ly/Acts2:42); [1 Cor 11:20–34](https://ref.ly/1Cor11:20-1Cor11:34)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 18:1-15
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 16:7 (ID: `12354@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); Tent (ID: `realia:Tent`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Mamre (ID: `place:Mamre`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Seah (ID: `realia:Seah`); Oak (ID: `flora:Oak`)

## Genesis 18:2–8 (id: 12374)

Abraham received his visitors as very important guests, perhaps realizing that they were messengers from God.

## Genesis 18:3 (id: 12378)

*My lord:* The Hebrew text uses *’adonay*, which always refers to God. In Hebrew tradition, people said this word when they saw the letters of God's holy name ("Yahweh") in the text. The text here might use *’adonay* instead of the more common *’adoni* to show this was the angel of the Lord, meaning the Lord himself (see study note on 16:7). We do not know if Abraham knew who his visitors were at first. But by the end of the story, he knew he had been talking with God.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 18:3
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 16:7 (ID: `12354@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`)

The visitors asked about Sarah by name, even though no one had told them who she was.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 18:9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Tent (ID: `realia:Tent`); Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`)

## Genesis 18:10 (id: 12369)

*I will surely return:* The Hebrew phrase is emphatic. The statement announced a certain promise and dramatic change.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 18:10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); Tent (ID: `realia:Tent`)

## Genesis 18:13–15 (id: 12370)

Sarah thought her disbelieving laughter was hidden, but God knows human hearts (see [Ps 69:5](https://ref.ly/Ps69:5); [Prov 20:27](https://ref.ly/Prov20:27); [Mark 4:22](https://ref.ly/Mark4:22); [Luke 8:43–48](https://ref.ly/Luke8:43-Luke8:48); [Heb 4:13](https://ref.ly/Heb4:13)), whether they stagger at the promises or step out in faith (see [Heb 11:11–12](https://ref.ly/Heb11:11-Heb11:12)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 18:13-15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 18:14 (id: 12371)

Is anything too hard for the Lord? The question is rhetorical. God is able to do marvelous things. Nothing is incredible to those in covenant fellowship with the Lord, because nothing is too difficult for him.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 18:14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 18:16–33 (id: 12372)

God took Abraham into his confidence as his prophet ([18:16–21](https://ref.ly/Gen18:16-Gen18:21); see [20:7](https://ref.ly/Gen20:7)); Abraham, in turn, interceded for Sodom ([18:23–32](https://ref.ly/Gen18:23-Gen18:32); see [Heb 7:23–26](https://ref.ly/Heb7:23-Heb7:26)). God is able to do whatever he chooses to do; this passage affirms that it will be just and right.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 18:16-33

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Gomorrah (ID: `place:Gomorrah`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); The Way (ID: `keyterm:TheWay`)

## Genesis 18:17–19 (id: 12373)

Abraham was responsible for teaching his descendants righteousness and justice so that they might enjoy God’s blessings. It was important for Abraham to know how God’s righteousness was at work in judgment.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 18:17-19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); The Way (ID: `keyterm:TheWay`)

## Genesis 18:20 (id: 12376)

*The outcry:* See [Ezekiel 16:49–50](https://ref.ly/Ezek16:49-Ezek16:50).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 18:20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gomorrah (ID: `place:Gomorrah`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`)

## Genesis 18:20–21 (id: 12375)

The omniscient God was cautious in his judgment: He knew the sins of Sodom and Gomorrah, but this close scrutiny communicated God’s justice in human terms—he would not destroy the people of the plain unless he was absolutely sure they were wicked.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 18:20-21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gomorrah (ID: `place:Gomorrah`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`)

## Genesis 18:22–33 (id: 12377)

Abraham probably thought there were more righteous people in Sodom and Gomorrah than there were (see study note on 19:14). In his concern for them, he approached the Lord with a legal appeal based on God’s justice. His prayer seems too bold at times, as though he were bargaining with God, but he approached God with genuine humility and reverence. He did not try to talk God into doing something against his will, but prayed for the well\-being of others (contrast Lot’s prayer, [19:18–23](https://ref.ly/Gen19:18-Gen19:23)). God is a righteous judge; righteousness exalts a nation ([Prov 14:34](https://ref.ly/Prov14:34)), and righteous people help to preserve society (cp. [Matt 5:13](https://ref.ly/Matt5:13)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 18:22-33
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 19:14 (ID: `12382@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`)

## Genesis 19:1 (id: 12385)

The two angels also first appeared to Abraham as men ([18:2](https://ref.ly/Gen18:2)). • Lot was no longer living in tents next to Sodom ([13:12](https://ref.ly/Gen13:12))—he had become a citizen and leader in Sodom, sitting there at the entrance of the city. Community leaders (elders) usually congregated in the gates, where legal and business transactions were publicly finalized (cp. [23:18](https://ref.ly/Gen23:18); [Job 29:7](https://ref.ly/Job29:7), [12–17](https://ref.ly/Job29:12-Job29:17)). As a righteous man ([2 Pet 2:7–8](https://ref.ly/2Pet2:7-2Pet2:8)), Lot tried to modify the townspeople’s wickedness by giving advice on good living (cp. [Gen 19:9](https://ref.ly/Gen19:9)). Although he denounced gross evil, Lot preferred Sodom’s sumptuous lifestyle to life in the hills (cp. [13:10–11](https://ref.ly/Gen13:10-Gen13:11)), where there was clean living but no “good life.” As long as the Lord left Lot and his family alone in Sodom, he lived comfortably there and kept his personal belief in God; but finally, he could not hold to both. Sodom would have destroyed Lot if the Lord had not destroyed Sodom. • This account showed Israel that God is the righteous judge of the whole earth ([18:25](https://ref.ly/Gen18:25)) who will judge evildoers with justice and equity. In wicked societies, moral and ethical failures lead to social injustice.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`); Lot (ID: `person:Lot`)

## Genesis 19:1–14 (id: 12380)

The two angels who were with the Lord at Mamre (cp. [18:2](https://ref.ly/Gen18:2), [22](https://ref.ly/Gen18:22)) visited Sodom reluctantly, knowing what kind of people lived there. Despite Lot’s hospitality, they preferred lodging in the square to entering Lot’s house.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:1-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lot (ID: `person:Lot`); House (ID: `realia:House`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`); Angels (ID: `group:Angels`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Housetop (ID: `realia:Housetop`); Unleavened Bread (ID: `realia:UnleavenedBread`)

## Genesis 19:1–38 (id: 12381)

The Canaanites were an evil, corrupting people. God judged their morally bankrupt civilization and warned others against becoming like them. It was difficult to get Lot and his family out of Sodom; it was more difficult to get Sodom out of Lot and his family. This chapter helped later Israelites to understand the moral and spiritual threat of the peoples living in and around the Promised Land, such as the Canaanites and Lot’s descendants, the Moabites and the Ammonites (see [Num 22–25](https://ref.ly/Num22:1-Num25:18); [Deut 23:3–6](https://ref.ly/Deut23:3-Deut23:6); [Josh 24:9](https://ref.ly/Josh24:9); [Judg 10:7–9](https://ref.ly/Judg10:7-Judg10:9); [11:4–5](https://ref.ly/Judg11:4-Judg11:5); [1 Sam 10:27](https://ref.ly/1Sam10:27); [1 Kgs 11:1–3](https://ref.ly/1Kgs11:1-1Kgs11:3); [2 Kgs 24:2](https://ref.ly/2Kgs24:2)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:1-38

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lot (ID: `person:Lot`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Zoar (ID: `place:Zoar`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Wine (ID: `realia:Wine`); Gomorrah (ID: `place:Gomorrah`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Ben-ammi (ID: `person:Ben-ammi`); Moab (ID: `person:Moab`); Angels (ID: `group:Angels`); Ammon (ID: `place:Ammon`); Ammonites (ID: `group:Ammon`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Moab (ID: `place:Moab`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Kiln (ID: `realia:Kiln`); Housetop (ID: `realia:Housetop`); Unleavened Bread (ID: `realia:UnleavenedBread`)

## Genesis 19:4–5 (id: 12392)

The evil actions of the men of the city were matched by the hypocrisy of Lot ([19:6–9](https://ref.ly/Gen19:6-Gen19:9)). The men acted in a wicked and violent way toward Lot’s visitors. Lot tried to protect his guests. However, he offered his daughters to the crowd instead. This action showed serious moral failure.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:4-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`); Lot (ID: `person:Lot`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Genesis 19:6–9 (id: 12393)

Lot opposed homosexuality and rape and rebuked their wicked plans, but he was hypocritically willing to sacrifice his daughters to fend off the townsmen’s vice. Lot had originally pitched his tent next to Sodom; now Sodom controlled his life.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:6-9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lot (ID: `person:Lot`); Housetop (ID: `realia:Housetop`)

## Genesis 19:9 (id: 12394)

The men of the city were enraged by Lot’s attempts to curtail their wickedness. Lot had apparently not condemned them before, since they were amazed that he now judged them.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lot (ID: `person:Lot`)

## Genesis 19:14 (id: 12382)

Lot’s warning words were not taken seriously because of his hypocrisy. It seemed that there would not be even ten righteous people in the city.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Lot (ID: `person:Lot`)

## Genesis 19:15–23 (id: 12383)

* Lot escaped God's judgment because of God's grace, but Lot was still hesitant to leave Sodom. Israel would forever remember Lot as someone who hesitated and needed angels to pull him to safety. The Lord was merciful and spared Lot because of Abraham ([18:23](https://ref.ly/Gen18:23); [19:29](https://ref.ly/Gen19:29)).
* Today, people who follow God often feel pressure to copy the wrong ways that many people live. God asks his followers to live differently from people who do not follow him. This means Christians should choose actions and behaviors that show they belong to God ([1 John 2:15–17](https://ref.ly/1John2:15-1John2:17)). God will judge the corrupt systems of the world. This judgment will be even more severe than the destruction of Sodom and Gomorrah ([Matthew 11:23–24](https://ref.ly/Matt11:23-Matt11:24)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:15-23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lot (ID: `person:Lot`); Zoar (ID: `place:Zoar`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`)

## Genesis 19:18–22 (id: 12384)

Lot asked the angels for permission to live in the small town of Zoar (which means "little place") even after they saved him.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:18-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Zoar (ID: `place:Zoar`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Lot (ID: `person:Lot`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`)

## Genesis 19:23–25 (id: 12386)

Compare [Luke 17:29](https://ref.ly/Luke17:29). The eruption of Mount Vesuvius, the volcano that destroyed Pompeii in AD 79, along with more recent natural disasters, shows how quickly a complete disaster like this can happen.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:23-25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`); Gomorrah (ID: `place:Gomorrah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Zoar (ID: `place:Zoar`); Lot (ID: `person:Lot`)

## Genesis 19:26 (id: 12387)

looked back: The verb indicates prolonged, intense gazing toward the world she loved, not a curious glance ([15:5](https://ref.ly/Gen15:5); [Exod 33:8](https://ref.ly/Exod33:8); [Num 21:9](https://ref.ly/Num21:9); [1 Sam 2:32](https://ref.ly/1Sam2:32); cp. [Exod 3:6](https://ref.ly/Exod3:6)). Lot’s wife was too attached to Sodom to follow God’s call of grace, so she was included in the judgment as she lingered on the valley slopes. Christ’s return to judge the world will be as sudden and devastating as the destruction of Sodom ([Luke 17:32–37](https://ref.ly/Luke17:32-Luke17:37)). Those who crave the life of this wicked world will lose this world and the next.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:26


## Genesis 19:29 (id: 12388)

God listened to Abraham's plea (compare [18:23–32](https://ref.ly/Gen18:23-Gen18:32)). But Lot lost everything during the destruction of Sodom. His choice to live in the fertile land of Sodom eventually led him into a dangerous situation ([13:10–13](https://ref.ly/Gen13:10-Gen13:13)). After this, he could no longer remain in the land he had chosen.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lot (ID: `person:Lot`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 19:30–35 (id: 12389)

The character of Lot’s daughters was formed by Sodom’s culture more than by their father’s heritage, so they had no qualms about having children by their drunk father (cp. [9:21–22](https://ref.ly/Gen9:21-Gen9:22)). They saw no other way to carry on their line.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:30-35

## Genesis 19:30–38 (id: 12390)

The poverty of the cave contrasts with the wealth Lot shared with Abram and the good life he lived in Sodom. Abraham would father a righteous nation ([17:1](https://ref.ly/Gen17:1)), but Lot and his daughters gave birth to a new Sodom.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:30-38

## Genesis 19:36–38 (id: 12391)

The daughters’ plan worked, and they each became pregnant by their own father. From these two incest\-born sons came two perennial enemies of Israel, the nations of Moab and Ammon. Their grotesque wickedness was due in part to their origin. • Both daughters chose ambiguous names that hinted at their actions without raising the suspicions of those who did not know the stories.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 19:36-38

## Genesis 20:1 (id: 12423)

Gerar was near the coast in Philistine land, about 19 kilometers (12 miles) south of Gaza and 80 kilometers (50 miles) southwest of Hebron.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 20:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gerar (ID: `place:Gerar`); Shur (ID: `place:Shur`); Negev (ID: `place:Negeb`); Kadesh-barnea (ID: `place:Kadesh-barnea`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 20:1–18 (id: 12419)

This second “sister story” in Genesis (cp. [12:10–20](https://ref.ly/Gen12:10-Gen12:20)) occurred shortly before Sarah became pregnant with Isaac (ch [21](https://ref.ly/Gen21:1-Gen21:34)). On both occasions, God protected Abraham and Sarah’s marriage in purity for the sake of the covenant promises. Participation in God’s plan requires separation from worldly corruption. • This story took place in the Promised Land; it showed Israel how God intervened in people’s lives to fulfill his plan, how God continued to protect them against threats from other tribes, and how God used his chosen people to mediate his relationship with the nations. • God’s preventing the destruction of Abraham’s marriage by adultery reminded the Israelites to keep their marriages morally and racially pure ([Ezra 9:1–4](https://ref.ly/Ezra9:1-Ezra9:4); [Neh 13:23–27](https://ref.ly/Neh13:23-Neh13:27); [Mal 2:10–17](https://ref.ly/Mal2:10-Mal2:17)); they should not allow any opportunity for temptation ([Exod 20:14](https://ref.ly/Exod20:14), [17](https://ref.ly/Exod20:17); [Lev 20:10](https://ref.ly/Lev20:10); [21:13–15](https://ref.ly/Lev21:13-Lev21:15)). Adultery would eventually destroy the covenant and the covenant people.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 20:1-18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); Gerar (ID: `place:Gerar`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Shur (ID: `place:Shur`); Negev (ID: `place:Negeb`); Innocence (ID: `keyterm:Innocence`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Kadesh-barnea (ID: `place:Kadesh-barnea`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Genesis 20:2 (id: 12424)

Abraham told the same lie to Abimelech that he had told to Pharaoh ([12:13](https://ref.ly/Gen12:13)); Isaac would later do the same ([26:1–11](https://ref.ly/Gen26:1-Gen26:11)), probably having learned this tactic from his father. • Abimelech (literally *my father the king*) was probably a title like “Pharaoh” ([37:36](https://ref.ly/Gen37:36); [Exod 1:15](https://ref.ly/Exod1:15)), not a proper name (see study note on Gen 26:1).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 20:2
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 26:1 (ID: `12533@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gerar (ID: `place:Gerar`); Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 20:3 (id: 12426)

that night God came . . . in a dream: God urgently intervened to stop Abimelech from violating Sarah’s purity shortly before God’s promise was fulfilled ([18:10](https://ref.ly/Gen18:10); [21:1–3](https://ref.ly/Gen21:1-Gen21:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 20:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 20:3–7 (id: 12425)

God gave Abimelech a stern warning against committing adultery; it was a capital offense (cp. [Exod 20:14](https://ref.ly/Exod20:14)), viewed throughout the ancient Near East as a “great sin” ([Gen 20:9](https://ref.ly/Gen20:9)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 20:3-7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); Innocence (ID: `keyterm:Innocence`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`)

## Genesis 20:4–5 (id: 12427)

Abimelech was innocent ([20:6](https://ref.ly/Gen20:6)); his conscience was clear. Nonetheless, he was about to commit adultery, and ignorance does not excuse guilt ([Lev 4:13–14](https://ref.ly/Lev4:13-Lev4:14)). • will you destroy an innocent nation? Abimelech’s appeal to God echoes Abraham’s earlier words (cp. [18:23–32](https://ref.ly/Gen18:23-Gen18:32)) and rebukes Abraham’s lack of faith on this occasion.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 20:4-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Innocence (ID: `keyterm:Innocence`); Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 20:6 (id: 12428)

Because Abimelech acted with a clear conscience, God kept him from sinning. God will graciously help those who try to do what is right. When people act with reverence toward God, God gives them more revelation and draws them into more specific faith (see [Acts 10](https://ref.ly/Acts10:1-Acts10:48)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 20:6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 20:7 (id: 12429)

Abraham's prayer saved the king's life and restored his family ([20:17–18](https://ref.ly/Gen20:17-Gen20:18)). Abimelech learned that Abraham's God was sovereign (meaning God has complete power and authority over everything). Abraham, as God's prophet, received God's messages and would pray for others (see [Numbers 12:13](https://ref.ly/Num12:13); [Deuteronomy 9:20](https://ref.ly/Deut9:20)). This was true even if he did not always act like a prophet.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 20:7


## Genesis 20:8–10 (id: 12430)

Abraham had earned rebukes from Abimelech and from God (cp. [12:17–19](https://ref.ly/Gen12:17-Gen12:19)). Abimelech was angry that Abraham’s deception had made him guilty of this great sin (see study note on 20:3–7). He knew that taking a married woman into his harem was wrong.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 20:8-10
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 20:3-7 (ID: `12425@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`)

## Genesis 20:11–13 (id: 12420)

Abraham’s duplicity was not a momentary loss of faith. Despite the rebuke he received in Egypt, he practiced this strategy wherever he went (cp. [12:12–13](https://ref.ly/Gen12:12-Gen12:13)). Living by faith requires perseverance.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 20:11-13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 20:14–16 (id: 12421)

Abimelech showed he was an honorable person by making things right (see study note on 20:4–5). He made things right by letting Abraham live in the area. He also gave him slaves, livestock (compare [21:27](https://ref.ly/Gen21:27)), and 1,000 pieces of silver to make up for any wrong done to Sarah.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 20:14-16
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 20:4-5 (ID: `12427@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Genesis 21:6 (id: 12445)

Sarah’s wordplay on the name *Isaac* (Hebrew yitskhaq, “he laughs”) shows that the laughter of unbelief when the promise was given ([18:12](https://ref.ly/Gen18:12)) had changed to the laughter of joy at its fulfillment. Isaac’s name could refer to the pleasure of God and of his parents at his birth. Sarah knew that everyone who heard about this would laugh with her and rejoice at the news.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 21:6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 21:6–7 (id: 12444)

Sarah was filled with joy and praise for this amazing event. Only God could enable her to have a child.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 21:6-7

## Genesis 21:8–9 (id: 12447)

The feast for Isaac’s weaning probably occurred when he was three and Ishmael was about seventeen years old ([16:16](https://ref.ly/Gen16:16)). Sarah saw Ishmael making fun of her son, Isaac: The verb metsakheq (“making fun of”) is related to the word for “laughter”; this theme ([21:6](https://ref.ly/Gen21:6)) is given a sour twist by Ishmael’s mockery.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 21:8-9

## Genesis 21:8–21 (id: 12446)

God used the incident of Ishmael’s mocking Isaac to separate Ishmael and Hagar from the family and the child of promise. They would constantly threaten the promised descendant if they remained with the family.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 21:8-21

## Genesis 21:10 (id: 12432)

Earlier, Sarah mistreated Hagar and pressured her to flee ([16:6](https://ref.ly/Gen16:6)); when Hagar’s son mistreated Isaac, Sarah demanded that that slave woman and her son leave.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 21:10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Dispossess (ID: `keyterm:Dispossess`)

## Genesis 21:11–13 (id: 12433)

Abraham was upset by Sarah’s demand to oust Hagar and Ishmael. God told him to comply, assuring Abraham that Ishmael would also have a future as Abraham’s offspring.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 21:11-13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 21:14–21 (id: 12434)

God again rescued Hagar in the wilderness and guaranteed her future (cp. [16:7–14](https://ref.ly/Gen16:7-Gen16:14)). This passage is similar to ch [16](https://ref.ly/Gen16:1-Gen16:16), but the differences are great. Here, Hagar and Ishmael are rescued, but there is no commemorative naming. God’s earlier promise to Hagar is reiterated, but this time Hagar is not told to return to Sarah. The repeated motifs on the two occasions confirm God’s sovereign plan for Hagar and Ishmael. As Joseph later told Pharaoh, a twofold event demonstrated God’s confirmation ([41:32](https://ref.ly/Gen41:32)). God did not abandon Hagar and Ishmael but met them in their despair (cp. [16:7](https://ref.ly/Gen16:7)), provided sustenance for them, and promised again that Ishmael would found a great nation ([21:13](https://ref.ly/Gen21:13); cp. [16:11–12](https://ref.ly/Gen16:11-Gen16:12)). Paul uses this event in his letter to the Galatians to illustrate how God’s people must relinquish all that threatens the fulfillment of God’s promise ([Gal 4:21–31](https://ref.ly/Gal4:21-Gal4:31)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 21:14-21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Skin-Bottle (ID: `realia:Skin-bottle`); Hagar (ID: `person:Hagar`); Bow (ID: `realia:Bow`); Paran (ID: `place:Paran`); Beersheba (ID: `place:Beersheba`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 21:16 (id: 12435)

*a bowshot away:* This means the distance an arrow can travel when someone shoots a bow. The phrase connects with Ishmael’s future work as an archer ([21:20](https://ref.ly/Gen21:20)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 21:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bow (ID: `realia:Bow`)

## Genesis 21:22–23 (id: 12436)

Abimelech pressed for the treaty so that Abraham would not cheat or deceive him. Abimelech knew that God was blessing Abraham even though Abraham was not entirely trustworthy ([20:9–10](https://ref.ly/Gen20:9-Gen20:10)). This sad contradiction made the treaty necessary. By contrast, God’s faithful people are exhorted to speak the truth ([Eph 4:15](https://ref.ly/Eph4:15), [25](https://ref.ly/Eph4:25)), and Jesus warned against manipulating truth by the clever use of oaths ([Matt 5:37](https://ref.ly/Matt5:37); [Jas 5:12](https://ref.ly/Jas5:12)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 21:22-23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Phicol (ID: `person:Phicol`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 21:22–34 (id: 12437)

This passage, at its climax, explains the name of Beersheba, Abraham’s home ([21:31–34](https://ref.ly/Gen21:31-Gen21:34)). Beersheba reflected the covenant Abraham made with the residents of the land, which enabled him to dwell there in peace and prosperity. God’s promise was coming to fruition ([12:7](https://ref.ly/Gen12:7); [13:14–17](https://ref.ly/Gen13:14-Gen13:17); [15:7](https://ref.ly/Gen15:7), [18–21](https://ref.ly/Gen15:18-Gen15:21); [17:8](https://ref.ly/Gen17:8)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 21:22-34

## Genesis 21:25 (id: 12438)

The motif of the well appears again (cp. [16:14](https://ref.ly/Gen16:14); [21:19](https://ref.ly/Gen21:19)). God provided water (a symbol of blessing) in the barren wilderness and later even brought water out of a rock for Israel ([Exod 15:22–27](https://ref.ly/Exod15:22-Exod15:27); [17:1–7](https://ref.ly/Exod17:1-Exod17:7); [Num 20:1–13](https://ref.ly/Num20:1-Num20:13)).

Abraham’s gifts to Abimelech (cp. [20:14](https://ref.ly/Gen20:14)) secured his legal right to dwell peaceably in the land and to claim ownership of the well. Beersheba marked one more step toward the fulfillment of God’s promise.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 21:27-31

## Genesis 21:32 (id: 12441)

The Philistines in Genesis are different from the Philistines of Judges through Kings. The earlier Philistines had Semitic names (e.g., Abimelech) and Canaanite culture. The later Philistines were apparently of Greek origin, with Greek customs and culture. They seem to have arrived in Canaan by sea from the Aegean area around 1200 BC, during the time of the judges. Probably the name of the later Philistines was used here simply to describe the region’s earlier inhabitants.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 21:32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Phicol (ID: `person:Phicol`); Beersheba (ID: `place:Beersheba`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`)

## Genesis 21:33–34 (id: 12442)

A tamarisk tree requires a lot of water; this act indicated Abraham’s security in his land rights and his faith that God would provide water in this desert area. He settled as a foreigner in the land, but dwelling under his tree was a sign of peaceful security (cp. [Zech 3:10](https://ref.ly/Zech3:10)). • there he worshiped the Lord: See study note on 12:8.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 21:33-34
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 12:8 (ID: `12306@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

## Genesis 22:1 (id: 12457)

Some time later: Abraham had sent Ishmael away and settled in the land. Now God tested Abraham’s faith by telling him to give up Isaac. This pushed the limits of logic and of Abraham’s knowledge of God. Would he still obey when God seemed to be working against him and against the covenant? Would he cling to the boy or surrender him to God (see [Exod 13:11–13](https://ref.ly/Exod13:11-Exod13:13))? Did he believe that God would still keep his word and bless the world through Abraham’s offspring?

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 22:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 22:1–2 (id: 12448)

The greatest test in Abraham’s life came after he had received the promised child following a long wait. He had grown to love Isaac and had enjoyed his presence for a number of years.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 22:1-2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Mount Moriah (ID: `place:MoriahMount`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 22:2 (id: 12462)

Obedience to God’s earlier call ([12:1–3](https://ref.ly/Gen12:1-Gen12:3)) was rewarded with great blessing; now Abraham had the opportunity to show even greater obedience. • Take your son . . . Isaac, whom you love so much: By this detailed description of Isaac, God reminded Abraham that the young man was his beloved son, and intensified his awareness of the cost of the sacrifice. • The name Moriah is explained by the Chronicler ([2 Chr 3:1](https://ref.ly/2Chr3:1)) as the place of the later Temple Mount in Jerusalem. • Go: By wording the command in this way, God helped Abraham to obey by recalling his former call (cp. [12:1–3](https://ref.ly/Gen12:1-Gen12:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 22:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Mount Moriah (ID: `place:MoriahMount`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`)

## Genesis 22:3 (id: 12463)

Abraham’s immediate, unquestioning obedience is almost as astounding as the test.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 22:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); To Saddle (ID: `realia:ToSaddle`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`)

## Genesis 22:5 (id: 12464)

We will worship there, and then we will come (or *We will worship there so that we may come back*): Abraham’s amazing statement makes us wonder what he was thinking. Abraham knew that God had planned the future of the covenant around Isaac and that God wanted him to sacrifice Isaac. He could not reconcile these things in his mind; he could only do what God commanded him to do, and leave the future to God (cp. [Heb 11:17–19](https://ref.ly/Heb11:17-Heb11:19)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 22:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`)

## Genesis 22:7–8 (id: 12465)

In response to Isaac’s question, Abraham again showed his faith in the Lord, saying God will provide*,* although he was not sure how. This theme is central to the entire narrative.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 22:7-8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 22:9–19 (id: 12466)

God’s intervention was dramatic and instructive, confirming that he never intended for Isaac to be sacrificed. God later made it clear that child sacrifice was an abomination to him (see [Lev 18:21](https://ref.ly/Lev18:21); [20:1–5](https://ref.ly/Lev20:1-Lev20:5); [Deut 18:10](https://ref.ly/Deut18:10); [2 Kgs 16:2–3](https://ref.ly/2Kgs16:2-2Kgs16:3); [Isa 57:5](https://ref.ly/Isa57:5); [Jer 32:35](https://ref.ly/Jer32:35)). God wanted Abraham to sacrifice his own will and surrender it to God, and when he did, God intervened. This passage sets a pattern for all sacrificial worshipers. Like Abraham, true worshipers of God know that everything belongs to God—it all came from God and must therefore be acknowledged as God’s possession. A true worshiper holds nothing back but obediently gives God what he asks, trusting that God will provide for all needs, and then discovering through experience that God always does so.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 22:9-19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Beersheba (ID: `place:Beersheba`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Dispossess (ID: `keyterm:Dispossess`); A Person (ID: `person:GenericPerson`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Stone Altar (ID: `realia:StoneAltar`); Temple Altar (ID: `realia:TempleAltar`); Knife (ID: `realia:Knife`); Altar (ID: `realia:Altar`); Tabernacle Altar (ID: `realia:TabernacleAltar`)

The angel of the Lord stopped Abraham just as he was ready to plunge the knife into his son.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 22:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`)

## Genesis 22:12 (id: 12450)

Now God knew that Abraham would hold nothing back from him, that he did truly fear God. To fear the Lord means to reverence him as sovereign, trust him implicitly, and obey him without protest. The sacrifice that pleases God is a heart broken of self\-will, surrendered to God ([Pss 40:6–8](https://ref.ly/Ps40:6-Ps40:8); [51:17](https://ref.ly/Ps51:17)) and offering its best to God. • You have not withheld: Cp. [Rom 8:32](https://ref.ly/Rom8:32), which uses the same verb (“spare”) as the Greek Old Testament uses here. If God gave us his dearest possession, he will surely provide all things for us.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 22:12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 22:13 (id: 12451)

God provided a ram caught by its horns in a thicket for the sacrifice. God graciously allowed Abraham to substitute an animal sacrifice in place of Isaac. Later, all Israel would offer animals to the Lord, knowing that God’s grace had provided this substitution ([Exod 29:10](https://ref.ly/Exod29:10); [Lev 4:15](https://ref.ly/Lev4:15); [16:20–22](https://ref.ly/Lev16:20-Lev16:22)). In the New Testament, God substituted his only son for all humanity; the perfect sacrifice was made once and for all ([Isa 53:6](https://ref.ly/Isa53:6), [10](https://ref.ly/Isa53:10); [John 1:29](https://ref.ly/John1:29); [Heb 7:27](https://ref.ly/Heb7:27); [10:1–14](https://ref.ly/Heb10:1-Heb10:14); [1 Pet 3:18](https://ref.ly/1Pet3:18)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 22:13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 22:14 (id: 12452)

As with many patriarchal narratives, the heart of the matter is retained through commemorative naming. Yahweh\-Yireh . . . means “the Lord will provide” (see [22:8](https://ref.ly/Gen22:8)). That Abraham used the holy name in this act shows that the patriarchs knew the name Yahweh (cp. [Exod 6:2–3](https://ref.ly/Exod6:2-Exod6:3)) but not its full meaning (see study note on Exod 6:2–3). • To this day: Later Israelites understood this passage as a lesson about their own worship in Jerusalem. Abraham’s sacrifice took place on the mountain of the Lord, later the location of the Temple in Jerusalem (see study note on 22:2). Three times a year, the people of Israel brought their best to God as a sacrifice, trusting that he would continue to provide for their needs.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 22:14
* **Associated Articles:** Exodus 6:2-3 (ID: `11409@TyndaleStudyNotes`); Genesis 22:2 (ID: `12462@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** A Person (ID: `person:GenericPerson`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

[32:12](https://ref.ly/Gen32:12)), and like the dust of the earth (cp. [13:16](https://ref.ly/Gen13:16); [28:14](https://ref.ly/Gen28:14)).

## Genesis 23:1–20 (id: 12467)

When Sarah . . . died, Abraham acquired a parcel of land for a burial place. This transaction was the first sign that a permanent transition had taken place, as people were normally buried in their ancestral homeland (cp. [49:29–50:13](https://ref.ly/Gen49:29-Gen50:13)). In burying Sarah, Abraham detached from his just\-mentioned ancestral home (where his relatives still lived, [22:20–24](https://ref.ly/Gen22:20-Gen22:24)); his future would be in Canaan, where his descendants would realize the promise.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 23:1-20

## Genesis 23:3–4 (id: 12473)

Abraham bargained with local Hittite elders for a piece of land for a burial site. These Hittites had apparently migrated south to Canaan from the great Hittite empire in eastern Asia Minor (modern Turkey; cp. [10:15](https://ref.ly/Gen10:15)). • Abraham was a stranger and a foreigner among these people; his hope was in God’s promise that he would eventually possess the land.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 23:3-4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Heth (ID: `person:Heth`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 23:5–6 (id: 12474)

my lord, you are an honored prince: Either Abraham was highly regarded by these people, or they were politely appealing to his generosity. • Choose the finest. . . . No one here will refuse: They were willing to accommodate his request, especially if they could legally obligate him to themselves ([23:11](https://ref.ly/Gen23:11)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 23:5-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Heth (ID: `person:Heth`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 23:7–16 (id: 12475)

Abraham wanted to buy Ephron’s cave at Machpelah, but Ephron wanted him to buy the entire field.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 23:7-16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ephron (ID: `person:Ephron.2`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Heth (ID: `person:Heth`); Shekel (ID: `realia:Shekel`); Machpelah (ID: `place:Machpelah`); Zohar (ID: `person:Zohar`); Hittites (ID: `group:Hittite`)

## Genesis 23:9 (id: 12476)

This would be a permanent burial place for Abraham’s family. The site was near Mamre ([23:19](https://ref.ly/Gen23:19)), where Abraham lived (see [13:18](https://ref.ly/Gen13:18); [14:13](https://ref.ly/Gen14:13); [18:1](https://ref.ly/Gen18:1)). Abraham and Sarah, Isaac and Rebekah, and Jacob and Leah would all be buried in this cave ([23:19](https://ref.ly/Gen23:19); [25:9](https://ref.ly/Gen25:9); [35:27–29](https://ref.ly/Gen35:27-Gen35:29); [49:29–31](https://ref.ly/Gen49:29-Gen49:31); [50:13](https://ref.ly/Gen50:13)), their permanent place in the Promised Land.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 23:9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Machpelah (ID: `place:Machpelah`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Genesis 23:11 (id: 12469)

Ephron did not intend to give the cave to Abraham; Abraham was expected to “give” the full price in return. Ephron wanted to sell as much as he could to avoid responsibility for caring for the cave and to receive as high a price as possible.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 23:11


## Genesis 23:12–13 (id: 12470)

Abraham did not want the whole field, but he was willing to take it to get the cave.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 23:12-13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Ephron (ID: `person:Ephron.2`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Genesis 23:15 (id: 12471)

*four hundred shekels of silver*: This was a high price. Ephron's politeness was part of the usual bargaining process.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 23:15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Shekel (ID: `realia:Shekel`)

## Genesis 23:16–20 (id: 12472)

Abraham paid the amount and finalized the transaction, avoiding indebtedness by accepting no gifts from the people (cp. [14:21–24](https://ref.ly/Gen14:21-Gen14:24)). The Hittite elders witnessed the transaction, ensuring that no one could challenge Abraham’s full ownership of the land. The transaction took place at the city gate, where public legal and business dealings were conducted (cp. [19:1](https://ref.ly/Gen19:1)). The land became Abraham’s permanent possession, a down payment on God’s promise to give him the land. Abraham knew that God’s promise was not fulfilled ([12:7](https://ref.ly/Gen12:7)) by this acquisition, so he planned for the future. By buying land for his dead, he declared that God’s promises do not end with this life. This is the hope of all who die in faith.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 23:16-20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Heth (ID: `person:Heth`); Mamre (ID: `place:Mamre`); Ephron (ID: `person:Ephron.2`); Machpelah (ID: `place:Machpelah`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Sarah (ID: `person:Sarah`); Hebron (ID: `place:Hebron`); Shekel (ID: `realia:Shekel`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Genesis 24:1–9 (id: 12478)

Confident in the Lord’s promise, Abraham had his chief servant (probably Eliezer, [15:2](https://ref.ly/Gen15:2)) swear to find a wife among Abraham’s relatives in his homeland, some 450 miles away. Abraham was faithful to the covenant by preparing for Isaac’s future.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 24:1-9

## Genesis 24:10 (id: 12481)

Aram\-naharaim means "Aram of the two rivers." It was also known as Paddan\-aram, which means "the field of Aram" (compare [25:20](https://ref.ly/Gen25:20)). 

The journey took two weeks each way. So, the servant took 10 of his master’s camels and departed with all kinds of gifts ([24:22](https://ref.ly/Gen24:22), [53](https://ref.ly/Gen24:53)).

## Genesis 24:14 (id: 12482)

Abraham’s future daughter\-in\-law showed hospitality and hard work, similar to Abraham (see [18:1–8](https://ref.ly/Gen18:1-Gen18:8)). Ten thirsty camels could drink 946 liters (250 gallons) of water. A woman willing to do that much work for a stranger was certainly not lazy, but generous and hospitable.

## Genesis 24:27 (id: 12488)

## Genesis 24:33–48 (id: 12491)

The servant was not diverted from his mission; he insisted on telling his story before he would eat. He recounted his mission and acknowledged God’s providence in directing him to Rebekah before attending to his personal needs. The servant wanted everyone to know that this was God’s work, not a chance or humanly arranged meeting.

## Genesis 24:48 (id: 12493)

The way that God directed this event from behind the scenes is different than in most of Genesis, but it is true to how the life of faith normally works. Faith, expressed in personal prayer and obedience, looks for evidence of God’s working. Believers usually have to make wise choices and remain faithful to the covenant, trusting that God will guide them through the circumstances of life to accomplish his will.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 24:48

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Truth (ID: `keyterm:Truth`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 24:54–56 (id: 12496)

It was hard for Rebekah’s family to let her go so suddenly, and Laban may have hoped to gain more wealth. However, the servant had sworn an oath and would not rest until it was completed. There was no reason for him to stay.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 24:54-56

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Mother of Rebekah (ID: `person:MotherOfRebekah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 24:57–58 (id: 12497)

Rebekah chose to leave at once to be with her new husband, which ended the disagreement. She accepted what seemed to be the Lord’s clear leading.

In that culture, young women usually wanted to marry (not marrying brought great shame and hardship). Later stories about Laban help explain why Rebekah may have wanted to leave (compare [31:14–15](https://ref.ly/Gen31:14-Gen31:15)). 

Rebekah showed faithful love to the servant, to Abraham’s family, and to Isaac by going to become his wife.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 24:57-58

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`)

## Genesis 24:60 (id: 12499)

When Rebekah left, her family blessed her, hoping she would become the mother of millions. They said, *May your* *offspring possess the gates of their enemies*. Rebekah's marriage to Isaac was part of God's plan to bless "all the families of the earth" ([12:1–3](https://ref.ly/Gen12:1-Gen12:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 24:60

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Dispossess (ID: `keyterm:Dispossess`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`)

## Genesis 25:5–6 (id: 12526)

Abraham loved all his sons, so before he died, he gave them gifts and sent them away as he had sent Ishmael ([21:8–14](https://ref.ly/Gen21:8-Gen21:14)). This made certain that Isaac remained his heir.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:5-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 25:7–8 (id: 12527)

Abraham’s death is recorded before the births of Jacob and Esau, but he lived until they were fifteen years old ([25:19–26](https://ref.ly/Gen25:19-Gen25:26); cp. [21:5](https://ref.ly/Gen21:5); [25:26](https://ref.ly/Gen25:26)). This literary arrangement closes Abraham’s story before focusing on Isaac’s family.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:7-8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 25:11 (id: 12503)

God’s blessing transferred to Isaac; Abraham’s other sons had been sent away. Isaac lived near Beer\-lahai\-roi—a special place where God had answered prayer ([16:14](https://ref.ly/Gen16:14)) and where Isaac waited on the Lord ([24:62](https://ref.ly/Gen24:62)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:11

## Genesis 25:12–18 (id: 12504)

This record lists Ishmael’s descendants before giving the details of Isaac’s descendants (see [25:19–35:29](https://ref.ly/Gen25:19-Gen35:29)). This follows the literary structure of Genesis (see Genesis Book Introduction, “Summary”).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:12-18

Ishmael's 12 sons fulfilled God's promise of blessings ([Genesis 17:20](https://ref.ly/Gen17:20)).

## Genesis 25:18 (id: 12506)

* Havilah was a region in north\-central Arabia.
* Shur was an area between Beersheba and Egypt.
* *And they lived in hostility toward all their brothers:* This wording is similar to [16:12](https://ref.ly/Gen16:12).
