## Genesis 25:19–20 (id: 12507)

*Isaac … married Rebekah:* Isaac’s marriage connected him and his family more closely to Abraham’s relatives. If he had married a Canaanite woman, the faith of the covenant could have been endangered by these corrupt people and their mixed worship.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:19-20

## Genesis 25:21 (id: 12512)

For twenty years, Rebekah was barren (cp. [25:20](https://ref.ly/Gen25:20), [26](https://ref.ly/Gen25:26)), like Sarah (see [16:1](https://ref.ly/Gen16:1)). This condition tested their faith (see study note on 16:1–3). How could they be childless when God promised that nations would issue from them? • Isaac pleaded with the Lord and the Lord answered Isaac’s prayer. Isaac apparently learned from his father’s mistake and responded in faith.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:21
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 16:1-3 (ID: `12348@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`)

## Genesis 25:22 (id: 12513)

When the pregnancy was difficult, Rebekah went to ask the Lord about it, probably by visiting a prophet—perhaps Abraham ([20:7](https://ref.ly/Gen20:7); see study note on 25:7–8).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:22
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 25:7-8 (ID: `12527@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

## Genesis 25:23 (id: 12514)

rivals: Jacob and Esau fought in the womb, and their descendants (Israel and Edom) fought continuously throughout their history. In their many conflicts, Israel achieved supremacy over Edom. • your older son will serve your younger son: God’s choice of the younger son over the elder ran against natural order (cp. [48:12–14](https://ref.ly/Gen48:12-Gen48:14); see [Mal 1:1–3](https://ref.ly/Mal1:1-Mal1:3); [Rom 9:11–16](https://ref.ly/Rom9:11-Rom9:16)).

## Genesis 25:24–26 (id: 12515)

When the twins were born, the unusual circumstances inspired each boy’s name and hinted at what would happen to them in the future.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:24-26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Clothing (ID: `realia:Clothing`)

## Genesis 25:25 (id: 12516)

Two wordplays anticipate Esau’s later life. Esau sounds like Hebrew se‘ar, which means “hair”; Esau’s later homeland, Edom, was known as *Seir* (“hairy”) because it was wooded (as though covered with hair). • red (Hebrew ’admoni) sounds like Esau’s other name, Edom ([25:27–34](https://ref.ly/Gen25:27-Gen25:34)); Edom had red soil. • Esau’s \>hair was like the fur coat of an animal, foreshadowing his unspiritual character ([25:34](https://ref.ly/Gen25:34); [Heb 12:16](https://ref.ly/Heb12:16); cp. [Lev 26:22](https://ref.ly/Lev26:22); [Deut 7:22](https://ref.ly/Deut7:22); [1 Cor 15:32](https://ref.ly/1Cor15:32)). The description of the child uses words that highlight the Edomites’ nature.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Clothing (ID: `realia:Clothing`)

## Genesis 25:26 (id: 12517)

The name Jacob (Hebrew ya‘aqob, from Hebrew ‘aqeb, “heel, footprint”) was originally positive, meaning “protect” (like a rear guard), but it took on the negative meaning of “heel grabber” or “deceiver” in the context of Jacob’s deceptive, grasping, usurping character (see [27:36](https://ref.ly/Gen27:36)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 25:27 (id: 12519)

Esau was a skillful hunter and an outdoorsman—a wild man who loved wild country. Jacob had a quiet (or *even*) temperament and preferred to stay at home (literally *dwelling in tents*)—i.e., he was civilized.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Tent (ID: `realia:Tent`)

## Genesis 25:27–34 (id: 12518)

Jacob and Esau each developed in accord with his initial characteristics ([25:24–26](https://ref.ly/Gen25:24-Gen25:26)). Esau, the reddish, hairy man, cared about physical things rather than spiritual things (see [Heb 12:16](https://ref.ly/Heb12:16)); he was finally overcome by physical appetites and sold his birthright. Jacob, the heel grabber, knew the birthright’s value and drove a ruthless bargain to gain it presumptuously from his brother.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:27-34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Stew (ID: `realia:Stew`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Lentil (ID: `flora:Lentil`); Tent (ID: `realia:Tent`)

## Genesis 25:28 (id: 12520)

* Each parent had a favorite child.
* *Because:* Isaac loved Esau because of his hunting ability. Rebekah had a constant and unconditional love for Jacob.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`)

## Genesis 25:29 (id: 12521)

Ironically, Jacob proved the more cunning hunter. The word cooking (Hebrew wayyazed, “boiling”) sounds like the word for “hunter” (Hebrew tsayid). While boiling stew, Jacob was laying a trap for the hairy red animal. He may have waited a long time for this opportunity. This word for “boil” was also used for presumptuous action (like water boiling over the rim of the pot). Jacob overstepped his boundaries when he seized the promise for himself. By contrast, Abraham knew the promise was his and was secure in giving the land away (see study note on 13:8–13). • Esau was exhausted and hungry, but his life was not in danger ([25:32](https://ref.ly/Gen25:32); see study note on 25:33–34).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:29
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 13:8-13 (ID: `12316@TyndaleStudyNotes`); Genesis 25:33-34 (ID: `12524@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Stew (ID: `realia:Stew`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 25:30 (id: 12522)

Esau was preoccupied with his appetite. Being driven by one’s appetites leaves no place for spiritual values. The text emphasizes this by using a Hebrew word (translated give) that was normally used for feeding animals. • Edom, which means “red”: See study note on 25:25.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:30
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 25:25 (ID: `12516@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 25:31–33 (id: 12523)

Jacob was the better hunter on this occasion, but great danger lay in exercising such strong ambition. God’s people should desire the things of God, but they must not seek them in the flesh (see [Zech 4:6](https://ref.ly/Zech4:6); [Gal 5:16–17](https://ref.ly/Gal5:16-Gal5:17); [Eph 6:10–12](https://ref.ly/Eph6:10-Eph6:12)). The Lord dealt severely with Jacob to purge him of carnal methods. He later received the promise not as crafty Jacob the usurper, but as Israel (meaning, “God fights”), with God fighting on his behalf ([Gen 32:28](https://ref.ly/Gen32:28)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:31-33

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`)

## Genesis 25:33–34 (id: 12524)

Esau eagerly took Jacob’s bait and fell into the trap. He ate and left too quickly to have been near death ([25:32](https://ref.ly/Gen25:32)). The final comment on the passage explains that Esau showed contempt for his birthright, considering it worthless ([Heb 12:16](https://ref.ly/Heb12:16)). It is foolish to sacrifice spiritual blessings to satisfy physical appetites (cp. [Gen 3:6](https://ref.ly/Gen3:6)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:33-34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Lentil (ID: `flora:Lentil`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Stew (ID: `realia:Stew`)

## Genesis 26:1 (id: 12533)

This Abimelech is probably not the man in ch [20](https://ref.ly/Gen20:1-Gen20:18), for these events could have been 90 years apart. Possibly Abimelech was a dynastic name or title (a later King Achish, [1 Sam 21:10](https://ref.ly/1Sam21:10), was also called Abimelech, [Ps 34:title](https://ref.ly/Ps34:1)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:1

## Genesis 26:1–35 (id: 12528)

In this digression from Jacob’s story, Isaac’s prosperity (ch [26](https://ref.ly/Gen26:1-Gen26:35)) shows that the blessing had passed to him (cp. [25:11](https://ref.ly/Gen25:11)) despite his failures of faith.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:1-35

## Genesis 26:2–5 (id: 12534)

The Lord assured Isaac that the covenant promises (cp. [12:2–3](https://ref.ly/Gen12:2-Gen12:3); [15:5–8](https://ref.ly/Gen15:5-Gen15:8); [17:3–8](https://ref.ly/Gen17:3-Gen17:8); [22:15–18](https://ref.ly/Gen22:15-Gen22:18); [28:13–14](https://ref.ly/Gen28:13-Gen28:14)) would pass to him because Abraham faithfully listened to God and obeyed all his requirements, commands, decrees, and instructions. These terms were later used in Deuteronomy to describe God’s full legal covenant with Israel. An Israelite reader would immediately think of the complete Torah when hearing these words and be prompted to obey God’s law as Abraham did, though Abraham had only a few commands from the Lord. Through these words, the text emphasizes that Abraham would have obeyed the later commands if he had had them, because he was an obedient servant of the Lord.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:2-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Oath (ID: `keyterm:Oath`); Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`)

## Genesis 26:6–11 (id: 12539)

While staying in Gerar, Isaac, like his father, deceived people into believing that his wife was his sister. Some suppose that this story duplicates the stories of Abraham’s deception ([12:10–20](https://ref.ly/Gen12:10-Gen12:20); [20:1–18](https://ref.ly/Gen20:1-Gen20:18)), but the differences are greater than the similarities, and the son’s repetition of his father’s lie is natural. Through numerous parallels with Abraham, ch [26](https://ref.ly/Gen26:1-Gen26:35) shows how God’s plan continued with Isaac. Even when Isaac jeopardized the covenant as his father had, God prevented disaster and preserved the marriage. Abraham’s descendants would be blessed because of Abraham, but they had to exercise their own faith to enjoy the blessings. Genuine faith in God’s promises engenders a fearless walk with him; cowering in fear endangers the blessing and mocks the faith.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:6-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Gerar (ID: `place:Gerar`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Window (ID: `realia:Window`)

## Genesis 26:8 (id: 12540)

Abimelech . . . saw Isaac caressing Rebekah: The word for “caressing” (Hebrew metsakheq) is the same as the word used for Ishmael’s “making fun of” Isaac ([21:9](https://ref.ly/Gen21:9)); the word is related to the name “Isaac” (Hebrew yitskhaq). It is as though Isaac’s lapse of faith made fun of Abimelech and made a mockery of Rebekah and the great promise embodied in Isaac’s name.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Window (ID: `realia:Window`)

## Genesis 26:10–11 (id: 12529)

Isaac, like his father, was rebuked by Abimelech (see study note on 20:1–18). This legal wording would remind Israel of how important it was to preserve marital purity. Abimelech recognized the danger to his own people. Though his decree preserved his own society, it was also a word from God that preserved the sanctity of Isaac’s and Israel’s marriages. If Isaac’s marriage had ended here, there would have been no Israelite society.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:10-11
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 20:1-18 (ID: `12419@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`)

## Genesis 26:12–13 (id: 12530)

Isaac lived in the land as a temporary resident. God blessed him, so his crops grew well and he became very rich.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:12-13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 26:14–16 (id: 12531)

The Philistines were jealous of Isaac's success and filled his wells with dirt. The king then told Isaac to leave the area because he was too powerful for them (compare [21:22–23](https://ref.ly/Gen21:22-Gen21:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:14-16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Be Zealous (ID: `keyterm:Zealous`); Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Genesis 26:17–22 (id: 12532)

Isaac moved . . . to the Gerar Valley (away from the city of Gerar itself, [26:6](https://ref.ly/Gen26:6), but probably still within ten miles) and reopened his father’s wells. Isaac was also opposed there, but chose not to fight back; he relinquished one well after another until God’s blessing outdid the opposition. Whenever Isaac reopened a well, and regardless of how often enemies caused them to cave in, he found water. God was blessing Isaac and that blessing could not be hindered. Finally, the Philistines left Isaac alone.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:17-22

## Genesis 26:26–33 (id: 12536)

This treaty is similar to the one an earlier king had made with Abraham (cp. [21:22–31](https://ref.ly/Gen21:22-Gen21:31)). This king acknowledged that God was blessing Isaac and realized that a treaty with Isaac would benefit him. No opposition can hinder God’s blessing—it will flourish, and other nations will see it and seek peace with God’s people to share in the blessing.

## Genesis 26:34–35 (id: 12538)

Esau’s marriages illustrate how unfit he was to lead the covenant people into God’s blessings, and how foolish was Isaac’s later attempt to bless Esau ([27:1–40](https://ref.ly/Gen27:1-Gen27:40)). Esau later married a third wife in a vain attempt to do the right thing ([28:6–9](https://ref.ly/Gen28:6-Gen28:9)).

## Genesis 27:1–4 (id: 12542)

The first scene sets up the chapter’s crisis. Isaac knew of God’s oracle ([25:22–23](https://ref.ly/Gen25:22-Gen25:23)), yet he thwarted or ignored it by trying to bless Esau. • Isaac was old and turning blind: He was losing his senses, both physically and spiritually.

## Genesis 27:1–40 (id: 12541)

Jacob got his father Isaac’s blessing through deception. In this story, an entire family tries to carry out their responsibilities by physical means rather than by faith. Faith would have provided Rebekah and Jacob a more honorable solution to the crisis.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:1-40

Like Esau, Isaac allowed his palate to govern his heart (cp. [25:28–34](https://ref.ly/Gen25:28-Gen25:34)).

## Genesis 27:5 (id: 12558)

Esau agreed to Isaac’s plan, thus breaking the oath he had sworn to Jacob ([25:33](https://ref.ly/Gen25:33)).

## Genesis 27:5–17 (id: 12557)

The blessing seemed to be in jeopardy. In scene two, Rebekah and Jacob sought to achieve God’s blessing by deception, without faith or love. Rebekah planned to deceive the old man into thinking that he was blessing Esau when he was actually blessing Jacob.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:5-17

## Genesis 27:34–35 (id: 12550)

Esau was very bitter and angry enough to kill. After Jacob received their father’s blessing, Esau planned to take his brother’s life ([27:41](https://ref.ly/Gen27:41)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:34-35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`)

## Genesis 27:36 (id: 12551)

Esau began to realize Jacob’s true nature, saying he has cheated me (or *tripped me up,* or *deceived me)* twice, by taking the birthright (the right of inheritance, Hebrew bekorah; [25:27–34](https://ref.ly/Gen25:27-Gen25:34)) and by deceiving their father to receive the blessing (the spoken pronouncement of the inheritance, Hebrew berakah). • Esau’s assessment of Jacob was correct, but he failed to see his own ungodliness in these transactions (see study note on 27:5; [Heb 12:16](https://ref.ly/Heb12:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:36
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 27:5 (ID: `12558@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 27:37 (id: 12552)

There was no way to undo the blessing. Isaac had declared a message from God, who had made Jacob to be Esau’s master (see [Romans 9:11–13](https://ref.ly/Rom9:11-Rom9:13)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:37

All that remained for Esau was a promise of hardship and struggle (compare [3:17–19](https://ref.ly/Gen3:17-Gen3:19); [16:11–12](https://ref.ly/Gen16:11-Gen16:12)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:39-40

## Genesis 27:41–45 (id: 12554)

Rebekah and Jacob received the blessing, but Esau hated them for it. They also separated from one another. The Bible does not indicate that Rebekah and Jacob ever saw each other again. 

They gained nothing that God would not have given them anyway. Their actions came at the expense of their relationship. Jacob had to flee from home to escape Esau's vengeance.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:41-45

## Genesis 27:42–33.17 (id: 12555)

Jacob met the Lord on a road that Abraham had traveled 125 years earlier. The story in [27:42–33:17](https://ref.ly/Gen27:42-Gen33:17) follows a pattern (called a "chiasm") focusing on Jacob’s exile: 

* A: Jacob runs away from Esau ([27:42–28:9](https://ref.ly/Gen27:42-Gen28:9))

    + B: Angels of God meet Jacob at Bethel ([28:10–22](https://ref.ly/Gen28:10-Gen28:22))

    - C: Jacob lives in exile in Paddan\-aram ([29:1–31:55](https://ref.ly/Gen29:1-Gen31:55))
        + B’: Angels of God meet Jacob at Mahanaim ([32:1–2](https://ref.ly/Gen32:1-Gen32:2))
* A’: Jacob reconciles with Esau ([32:3–33:17](https://ref.ly/Gen32:3-Gen33:17)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:42-45

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Bilhah (ID: `person:Bilhah`); Paddan-aram (ID: `place:Paddan-aram`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Zilpah (ID: `person:Zilpah`); Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Camel (ID: `fauna:Camel`); Teraphim (ID: `deity:Teraphim`); Jegar-sahadutha (ID: `place:Jegar-sahadutha`); Gilead (ID: `place:Gilead`); Arameans (ID: `group:Aramean`); Seir (ID: `place:Seir`)

## Genesis 28:1–2 (id: 12559)

* Isaac stayed in the land, but Jacob had to leave. God would deal with Jacob through his uncle Laban (see study note on 29:1–31:55).
* Believers must remain spiritually pure by marrying other believers ([2 Corinthians 6:14–18](https://ref.ly/2Cor6:14-2Cor6:18)). The Canaanites had many groups and clans among their people through wars, treaties, and marriages (see [Genesis 34:20–23](https://ref.ly/Gen34:20-Gen34:23)).

    Abraham’s family was to resist such mixing (compare [24:3](https://ref.ly/Gen24:3); chapter [34](https://ref.ly/Gen34:1-Gen34:31)). They were supposed to marry within their clan to maintain the purity of the line and of the faith that identified them as the chosen people. The surest way to lose their distinctiveness was to intermarry with people of other tribal backgrounds and beliefs (see [Ezra 9–10](https://ref.ly/Ezra9:1-Ezra10:44); [Nehemiah 13:23–29](https://ref.ly/Neh13:23-Neh13:29)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:1-2
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 29:1-31.55 (ID: `12573@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bethuel (ID: `person:Bethuel`); Paddan-aram (ID: `place:Paddan-aram`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Genesis 28:3–5 (id: 12571)

Before Jacob departed, Isaac gave him a pure, legitimate blessing. He did not hold back, because he now knew what God wanted him to do. Isaac clearly passed on the blessing God Almighty (Hebrew *El\-Shaddai*; see [17:1](https://ref.ly/Gen17:1)) had given to Abraham and to him regarding prosperity and the land (compare [15:5](https://ref.ly/Gen15:5), [18–20](https://ref.ly/Gen15:18-Gen15:20)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:3-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Paddan-aram (ID: `place:Paddan-aram`); Arameans (ID: `group:Aramean`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Dispossess (ID: `keyterm:Dispossess`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Bethuel (ID: `person:Bethuel`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`)

Esau, the unchosen son still trying to please his father, married a woman from the unchosen line of Ishmael, which he thought would be more acceptable. He did not understand the uniqueness of the covenant family.

Even though Jacob used deceptive methods to get the blessing, God promised to protect and provide for him. The God of Abraham and Isaac was also the God of Jacob. This revelation greatly changed Jacob's perspective and brought faith into clearer focus.

## Genesis 28:11 (id: 12561)

Jacob set up camp in a safe spot at the base of a hill. He probably used a large stone more for protection than as a pillow.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:11


## Genesis 28:12–13 (id: 12562)

stairway (traditionally *ladder*): This word occurs nowhere else in Scripture. The imagery probably reminded readers of a staircase or ramp up the front of a ziggurat that signified communication between heaven and earth (see study note on 11:4). God initiated a divine communication between heaven and earth to guide and protect Jacob, the steward of his covenant ([28:13–15](https://ref.ly/Gen28:13-Gen28:15); see [Ps 91:11–15](https://ref.ly/Ps91:11-Ps91:15)). • Jesus said that he himself is the stairway between heaven and earth ([John 1:51](https://ref.ly/John1:51)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:12-13
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 11:4 (ID: `12280@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`)

## Genesis 28:12–15 (id: 12563)

The point of the vision was that God and his angels were with Jacob on his journey. God reiterated to Jacob the covenant promises made to Abraham and Isaac, promising him land, descendants numerous as the dust (cp. [13:16](https://ref.ly/Gen13:16); [22:17](https://ref.ly/Gen22:17)), and universal blessing through him (cp. [12:2–3](https://ref.ly/Gen12:2-Gen12:3); [15:5](https://ref.ly/Gen15:5), [18](https://ref.ly/Gen15:18); [17:3–8](https://ref.ly/Gen17:3-Gen17:8); [22:15–18](https://ref.ly/Gen22:15-Gen22:18); [35:11–12](https://ref.ly/Gen35:11-Gen35:12)). God also promised to be with Jacob and watch over him until he returned.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:12-15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 28:14 (id: 12564)

Jacob inherited Abraham’s entire covenant (see [22:17](https://ref.ly/Gen22:17)), which confirmed Isaac’s blessing ([28:3–4](https://ref.ly/Gen28:3-Gen28:4)) and showed that Jacob would live away from home for a time ([28:15](https://ref.ly/Gen28:15); see [15:12–16](https://ref.ly/Gen15:12-Gen15:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`)

## Genesis 28:15 (id: 12565)

I am with you: The promise of God’s presence meant that God would protect and provide for Jacob in a special way. God’s promise to be with his people is repeated throughout Scripture (see also [26:24](https://ref.ly/Gen26:24)), prompting a response of worship and confidence in those who have faith ([28:16–22](https://ref.ly/Gen28:16-Gen28:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:15


## Genesis 28:16–22 (id: 12566)

The second half of the passage gives Jacob’s response to the revelation. He felt reverential fear and awe in the Lord’s presence, and his acts of devotion became archetypes of Israel’s worship. When God graciously visits his people and promises to be with them and make them a blessing to the world, his people respond in faith with reverential fear, worship, offerings, and vows. They preserve their faith in memory for future worshipers.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:16-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`); Peace (ID: `keyterm:IntactState`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Vow (ID: `keyterm:Vow`); Anointing Oil (ID: `realia:AnointingOil`); Olive Oil (ID: `realia:OliveOil`)

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:18

*Bethel* later became a holy site for Israel (see [Judges 20:18–27](https://ref.ly/Judg20:18-Judg20:27); [1 Samuel 7:16](https://ref.ly/1Sam7:16); [10:3](https://ref.ly/1Sam10:3); [1 Kings 12:26–13:10](https://ref.ly/1Kgs12:26-1Kgs13:10); [2 Kings 2:2–3](https://ref.ly/2Kgs2:2-2Kgs2:3)).

## Genesis 28:20–22 (id: 12569)

In view of what God would do for him, Jacob vowed to do certain things for God. He believed the Lord’s words and responded in gratitude. Jacob’s vow influenced Israel’s way of making commitments to God in worship.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:20-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Peace (ID: `keyterm:IntactState`); Vow (ID: `keyterm:Vow`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Genesis 28:22 (id: 12570)

*I will surely You a tenth:* By paying a tithe (*a tenth*) as an act of worship, a person acknowledges that everything is a gift from God and belongs to God (see [14:19–20](https://ref.ly/Gen14:19-Gen14:20); [Numbers 18:21–32](https://ref.ly/Num18:21-Num18:32); [Deuteronomy 14:22–29](https://ref.ly/Deut14:22-Deut14:29); [2 Chronicles 31:5–6](https://ref.ly/2Chr31:5-2Chr31:6); [Malachi 3:7–12](https://ref.ly/Mal3:7-Mal3:12); [Matthew 23:23](https://ref.ly/Matt23:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Genesis 29:1 (id: 12579)

*Jacob resumed his journey:* The Hebrew text can be literally translated as he "lifted up his feet," suggesting he felt inspired. He continued his journey with renewed enthusiasm. This change in attitude came from the vision he received at Bethel. 

It was a wonderful revelation that God would protect and bless him. Now, he expected God's promises to come true, not only to escape from Esau. Jacob’s attitude had become positive and magnanimous to the point of being naive and vulnerable.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 29:1–31.55 (id: 12573)

These chapters tell how God kept his promise by abundantly blessing Jacob with family and possessions. God also disciplined Jacob, leaving him to struggle with Laban for many years. Laban was Jacob’s match in deception, and thus a means of correction. • The story of Jacob and Laban parallels Israel’s later sojourn in Egypt. Jacob struggled while serving his uncle but finally emerged with a large family (the founders of the twelve tribes) and great wealth. In Egypt, the Israelites suffered under their oppressors, but they also flourished, becoming a great nation of twelve large tribes and escaping with great riches.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:1-31:55

## Genesis 29:2–12 (id: 12580)

In contrast to the lazy, unhelpful shepherds ([29:7–8](https://ref.ly/Gen29:7-Gen29:8)), Jacob is portrayed as generous, industrious, and energetic. • Jacob . . . watered his uncle’s flock: Laban’s flocks would flourish under Jacob’s care.

*Jacob kissed Rachel:* Kissing relatives was an appropriate way to greet them ([29:13](https://ref.ly/Gen29:13); compare [Song 8:1](https://ref.ly/Song8:1)).

## Genesis 29:14 (id: 12577)

*You are indeed my own flesh and blood:* Laban welcomed Jacob into his house and treated him much like a son.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Laban (ID: `person:Laban`)

## Genesis 29:14–30 (id: 12576)

Jacob’s joyful prospect of marriage to the lovely Rachel became an occasion for Laban’s shrewdness and Jacob’s discipline. Jacob and his mother had deceived his father and brother to gain the blessing; now his mother’s brother deceived him. Jacob received a dose of his own duplicity through twenty years of labor, affliction, and deception in Laban’s service ([31:38](https://ref.ly/Gen31:38)). In God’s justice, people harvest what they plant ([Gal 6:7](https://ref.ly/Gal6:7)). Laban’s deception was perfectly designed to make Jacob aware of his own craftiness. God often brings people into the lives of believers to discipline them. But Jacob was tenacious, and God blessed him abundantly with a large family and many possessions ([30:25–43](https://ref.ly/Gen30:25-Gen30:43)) during this time of service.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:14-30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Work (ID: `keyterm:Work`); Bilhah (ID: `person:Bilhah`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`); Zilpah (ID: `person:Zilpah`)

## Genesis 29:18 (id: 12578)

Seven years of service was a high bride\-price in the ancient world, but Rachel was beautiful (like Sarah and Rebekah), and Jacob was in love with her.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 29:23–26 (id: 12581)

Like Isaac, Jacob was plied with food and wine (cp. [27:25](https://ref.ly/Gen27:25)), deprived of sight in the darkness (cp. [27:1](https://ref.ly/Gen27:1)), baffled by clothing (cp. [27:15](https://ref.ly/Gen27:15)), and misled by touch (cp. [27:23](https://ref.ly/Gen27:23)). The marriage had been consummated ([29:23](https://ref.ly/Gen29:23); see [2:24](https://ref.ly/Gen2:24)), so Jacob was bound to Leah. • It’s not our custom here to marry off a younger daughter ahead of the firstborn: Laban’s words are a reminder of what Jacob did when he, the younger son, pretended to be his older brother to gain the blessing (ch [27](https://ref.ly/Gen27:1-Gen27:46)). Now Leah, the older sister, pretended to be the younger sister to get a husband. God gave the deceiver a dose of his own deception as a discipline in his life.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:23-26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`); Zilpah (ID: `person:Zilpah`)

## Genesis 29:28–30 (id: 12582)

After the bridal week ended ([29:27](https://ref.ly/Gen29:27)), Jacob also received Rachel as his wife, though he then would have to work an additional seven years (cp. [31:41](https://ref.ly/Gen31:41)). Laban seemed to have gained the upper hand.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:28-30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Bilhah (ID: `person:Bilhah`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`)

## Genesis 29:30 (id: 12583)

Jacob loved Rachel much more than Leah: Favoritism was an ongoing cause of dysfunction in Jacob’s family (cp. [25:28](https://ref.ly/Gen25:28); [37:3](https://ref.ly/Gen37:3)). Jacob’s favoritism had lasting effects: his family was never together, and their descendants, the tribes of Israel, were rarely unified.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`)

## Genesis 29:31–35 (id: 12585)

Leah’s first four sons were born in rapid succession, but Rachel could not conceive. She was barren, like Sarah and Rebekah (cp. [16:1](https://ref.ly/Gen16:1); [25:21](https://ref.ly/Gen25:21); see also [1 Sam 1:1–28](https://ref.ly/1Sam1:1-1Sam1:28); [Luke 1:5–25](https://ref.ly/Luke1:5-Luke1:25)). • Each name is a memorable wordplay on Leah’s experience and hopes.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:31-35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Simeon (ID: `person:Simeon.5`); Reuben (ID: `person:Reuben`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Levi (ID: `person:Levi.3`)

## Genesis 29:31–30.24 (id: 12584)

The rivalry between these sisters explains much of the later rivalry among their sons, and then among the tribes, just as the rivalry between Jacob and Laban foreshadowed conflict between Israel and the Arameans of Damascus ([2 Sam 8:5–6](https://ref.ly/2Sam8:5-2Sam8:6); [10:8–19](https://ref.ly/2Sam10:8-2Sam10:19); [1 Kgs 20:1–34](https://ref.ly/1Kgs20:1-1Kgs20:34); [2 Kgs 5:1–8:29](https://ref.ly/2Kgs5:1-2Kgs8:29); [13:1–25](https://ref.ly/2Kgs13:1-2Kgs13:25); [Isa 7:1–9](https://ref.ly/Isa7:1-Isa7:9)). • God champions the cause of the poor and oppressed; he exalted Leah, the despised first wife, as the first to become a mother. Judah’s kingly tribe and Levi’s priestly line came through her despite Jacob’s favoritism for Rachel and her children. Despite the tension and jealousy resulting from Laban’s treachery and Jacob’s favoritism, God still built Jacob’s family and brought about the births of the tribal ancestors.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:31-30:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Bilhah (ID: `person:Bilhah`); Zilpah (ID: `person:Zilpah`); Mandrake (ID: `flora:Mandrake`); Reuben (ID: `person:Reuben`); Gad (ID: `person:Gad`); Dan (ID: `person:Dan`); Dinah (ID: `person:Dinah`); Asher (ID: `person:Asher`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Zebulun (ID: `person:Zebulun`); Naphtali (ID: `person:Naphtali`); Simeon (ID: `person:Simeon.5`); Issachar (ID: `person:Issachar`); Be Zealous (ID: `keyterm:Zealous`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Levi (ID: `person:Levi.3`); Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); Wheat (ID: `flora:Wheat`)

## Genesis 29:32 (id: 12586)

Reuben (Hebrew re’uben) sounds like the Hebrew for “He has seen my misery” (ra‘ah be‘onyi). His birth gave Leah consolation from God and hope for Jacob’s love. Jacob seems not to have seen her misery, but God did (cp. [16:14](https://ref.ly/Gen16:14); [24:62](https://ref.ly/Gen24:62); [25:11](https://ref.ly/Gen25:11)). The name was a reminder of God’s intervention.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Reuben (ID: `person:Reuben`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`)

## Genesis 29:33 (id: 12587)

Leah named her second son Simeon. The name suggests that she had cried out to the Lord and was heard (compare [16:11](https://ref.ly/Gen16:11)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:33

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Simeon (ID: `person:Simeon.5`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 29:34 (id: 12588)

Leah named her third son Levi because she hoped her husband would become attached to her after she had borne him three sons. The name *Levi* sounds like the Hebrew word for “attached” or “joined.” But this did not happen.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Levi (ID: `person:Levi.3`)

## Genesis 29:35 (id: 12589)

Leah accepted that Jacob would never love her. She named her fourth son Judah, saying, "I will praise the Lord." In Hebrew, *Judah* means "praised." She appeared to have stopped hoping for Jacob's love and found comfort in the Lord.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 30:1–2 (id: 12611)

In that culture, for a woman not to have children could feel like a kind of death (compare [1 Samuel 1](https://ref.ly/1Sam1:1-1Sam1:28)). Only God could enable Rachel to have children.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:1-2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Be Zealous (ID: `keyterm:Zealous`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 30:1–8 (id: 12612)

Rachel’s naming of sons through Bilhah does not reflect faith as Leah’s namings had. Rachel felt wronged over the marriage and her barrenness. The names of Bilhah’s sons reflect Rachel’s bitter struggle with her sister and her feeling of some victory.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:1-8

*Dan* means “he judged” or “he vindicated.” Rachel felt justified (Hebrew *dananni*) by Dan’s birth.

## Genesis 30:10–13 (id: 12613)

*Gad* (meaning "fortune") was the name of a god associated with fortune. *Asher* (meaning "happy") was the name of a god associated with luck. These names show Leah's pagan background, but there is no indication she believed in these gods.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:10-13

## Genesis 30:14–17 (id: 12614)

People believed *mandrakes* could increase sexual desire and help women become pregnant (see [Song 7:13](https://ref.ly/Song7:13)). Rachel hoped they would help her become pregnant, so she made a deal with Leah. Rachel would let Leah spend one night with Jacob in exchange for mandrakes. Yet, Leah became pregnant, not Rachel.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:14-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Mandrake (ID: `flora:Mandrake`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Reuben (ID: `person:Reuben`); Wheat (ID: `flora:Wheat`)

## Genesis 30:18 (id: 12615)

The name *Issachar* reflects this idea of being hired or receiving payment. It reminds readers of the events surrounding his birth. Leah says that Jacob was "hired” for the night ([Genesis 30:16](https://ref.ly/Gen30:16)). The Hebrew word *sekari* means “my hire.”

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Issachar (ID: `person:Issachar`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 30:19–20 (id: 12616)

*Zebulun* means "honor" or "gift," like a dowry or tribute. Leah believed God gave her Zebulun so her husband would honor her. She always hoped this would happen.

## Genesis 30:22–24 (id: 12618)

* Rachel finally had her own son, Joseph, thanks to God's intervention. His birth was not due to superstitions ([30:14–16](https://ref.ly/Gen30:14-Gen30:16)) or the custom of giving servants as wives.
* The Hebrew word translated as "taken away" (*’asap*) sounds similar to the name *Joseph* (*yosep*), which means "may he add." Rachel was happy about Joseph's birth and prayed for another son.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:22-24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`)

## Genesis 30:25–34 (id: 12619)

After working for fourteen years, Jacob asked Laban if he could return home. The two nomadic leaders negotiated politely but stayed cautious. Laban wanted more from Jacob, while Jacob wanted to earn his wages through selective breeding.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:25-34

## Genesis 30:27 (id: 12620)

I have become wealthy: God had prospered Laban through Jacob’s presence (see [22:18](https://ref.ly/Gen22:18)). Laban may have looked for omens, or simply have perceived what was happening. Since dark\-colored sheep ([30:32](https://ref.ly/Gen30:32)) were rare, a large number of them was considered an omen of God’s blessing.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`)

## Genesis 30:30–33 (id: 12622)

Jacob agreed that God had blessed Laban through him, so he made a plan to gain something for himself. He proposed for his wages the rare black and multicolored goats and the speckled and spotted sheep that were born.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:30-33

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 30:32 (id: 12623)

As Abraham had done with Lot ([13:9](https://ref.ly/Gen13:9)), Jacob gave Laban what he valued most. White sheep were more common and more valuable than dark or multicolored sheep; as a man of faith, Jacob was willing to take the rejects (cp. [1 Cor 1:26](https://ref.ly/1Cor1:26)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`)

## Genesis 30:34–36 (id: 12624)

Laban verbally agreed with Jacob’s plan, but he tried to prevent Jacob from accruing wealth by removing animals from the flock that would fulfill the agreement. • Laban’s deception with his goats reminds us of Jacob’s deception of Esau (cp. [27:9](https://ref.ly/Gen27:9); see study note on 29:14–30).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:34-36
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 29:14-30 (ID: `12576@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 30:37 (id: 12626)

*exposing the white inner wood:* This clever wordplay explains the entire section. When Jacob *exposed the white* (Hebrew *laban*) streaks of wood underneath, he played the "white" game (the *Laban* game) and won. By outsmarting Laban ("Whitey"), Jacob's flocks increased and he became successful.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:37

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Plane Tree (ID: `flora:PlaneTree`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Poplar (ID: `flora:Poplar`); Almond (ID: `flora:Almond`)

## Genesis 30:37–43 (id: 12625)

God blessed Jacob despite Laban’s duplicity. Not to be outwitted, Jacob used selective breeding to acquire a flock, following the traditional belief that peeled sticks influenced the kind of animal that would be born. The peeled branches seemingly made his animals produce streaked and spotted young; Jacob later acknowledged that God had prospered him ([31:7–12](https://ref.ly/Gen31:7-Gen31:12)). Jacob gained stronger animals for himself and weaker ones for Laban ([30:41–42](https://ref.ly/Gen30:41-Gen30:42)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:37-43

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Plane Tree (ID: `flora:PlaneTree`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Almond (ID: `flora:Almond`); Poplar (ID: `flora:Poplar`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); Camel (ID: `fauna:Camel`)

Laban now received due recompense for his treatment of Jacob. Laban’s attempt to defraud Jacob resulted in Jacob’s coming out ahead, because God was at work in his life.

## Genesis 31:3 (id: 12644)

* *the land of your fathers* was Canaan, to which God had called Abraham ([12:1–7](https://ref.ly/Gen12:1-Gen12:7); [17:8](https://ref.ly/Gen17:8)).
* *Go back … I will be with you:* See study notes on 26:2–5; 28:12–15. God protected Jacob (also called “Israel,” [32:28](https://ref.ly/Gen32:28)) as he led his family back to the land God promised them. Later, God brought Israel back to Canaan after many years of slavery in Egypt.

    This return had many similarities to this passage:

    + God defeated foreign gods and beliefs.
        + He used dreams for rescue and protection.
        + He gave victory over threats.
        + He set boundaries between nations and tribes (see [Deuteronomy 32:8](https://ref.ly/Deut32:8)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:3
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 26:2-5 (ID: `12534@TyndaleStudyNotes`); Genesis 28:12-15 (ID: `12563@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 31:4–13 (id: 12645)

Jacob explained to his wives how God had blessed him despite Laban’s opposition. He was not sure they would want to leave Laban and go to Canaan. He wanted to take a willing family, so he had to make an effective appeal. He rehearsed God’s leading and provision over the years and then told them that he had to keep the vow he had made at Bethel ([28:20–22](https://ref.ly/Gen28:20-Gen28:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:4-13

The women responded immediately that they would go with Jacob because God had blessed him. They were very willing to leave Laban, who had squandered their wealth (the property that would have provided for them). They knew that what God had given to Jacob would also be theirs.

## Genesis 31:19–20 (id: 12636)

Rachel stole her father’s household idols: Rachel probably wanted to regain some of the assets Laban had squandered; possibly she also worshiped idols (cp. [35:2–4](https://ref.ly/Gen35:2-Gen35:4)). To have the idols may have signified claiming the family inheritance, as customs in subsequent periods indicate. Laban apparently felt vulnerable without them. Whatever her reasons, Rachel’s theft almost brought disaster on the fleeing family when Laban caught up with them. • A wordplay shows that Rachel and Jacob were very much alike—Rachel stole (Hebrew wattignob) Laban’s household gods, and Jacob outwitted (Hebrew wayyignob, “stole the heart of, deceived”) Laban.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:19-20

## Genesis 31:22–23 (id: 12638)

The theft of the idols ([31:19](https://ref.ly/Gen31:19)) was probably the main reason that Laban and his men chased Jacob. It was one thing for Jacob to take his family and flocks—Laban probably still believed they were all his—but another matter entirely to take his household gods. Laban may have feared that Jacob would return someday to claim all of Laban’s estate. When he failed to find the gods, he asked for a treaty to keep Jacob away ([31:43–53](https://ref.ly/Gen31:43-Gen31:53)). • It took Laban seven days to catch up with Jacob.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:22-23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Gilead (ID: `place:Gilead`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 31:24 (id: 12639)

leave Jacob alone! (literally *Do not speak to Jacob either good or evil*): God commanded Laban not to take justice into his own hands. When we try to enact our own sense of good and evil apart from God’s command, we always do evil (see study note on 2:9).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:24
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 2:9 (ID: `12418@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

The God that Isaac feared (see textual note) was with Jacob ([31:3](https://ref.ly/Gen31:3)), had seen his hard work and faithfulness despite Laban’s abuse, and had rewarded Jacob. Laban’s dream only proved to Jacob that he was in the right.

The stone and the heap of stones were a monument to the border treaty between the two men, as a witness to future generations. Each man named the monument witness pile in his native language. It remained the perpetual border between Israel and the kingdom of Aram (Syria), two nations often at war.

## Genesis 31:50–53 (id: 12651)

The women and children would be much safer and better cared for with Jacob than they ever were with Laban.

## Genesis 32:9–12 (id: 12668)

Jacob’s prayer is a wonderful example of how to address God. He based his appeal on God’s will, reminding God of his relationship with him, his command for him to return to the land, and his promise ([32:9](https://ref.ly/Gen32:9)). He had a correct attitude of genuine humility and total dependence on God ([32:10](https://ref.ly/Gen32:10)). Finally, he asked that God rescue him from his brother, and he repeated God’s promises (see [22:17](https://ref.ly/Gen22:17)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:9-12

## Genesis 32:22–24 (id: 12654)

The Jabbok River flows westward to the Jordan Valley, dividing the region of Bashan on the north from Gilead on the south. Wordplays on Jacob’s name and character preserve the memory of this encounter. Jacob (Hebrew ya‘aqob), while at Jabbok (Hebrew yabboq), wrestled (Hebrew wayye’abeq). Through his fight with an adversary to receive the blessing, Jacob’s name would be changed, and his deceptive striving would partially give way to faith as his way of life.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:22-24

a man came: The narrative unfolds as the event did for Jacob. ## Genesis 32:27 (id: 12659)

What is your name? The Lord’s question was really about Jacob’s character, not his identity (cp. [3:9](https://ref.ly/Gen3:9); [4:9](https://ref.ly/Gen4:9)). By giving his name, Jacob confessed his nature, his way of doing things as “Heel\-grabber, Deceiver, Usurper.” Before God would bless him, he had to acknowledge who he was, and then God would change his identity.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 32:28 (id: 12660)

Jacob: See study note on 25:26. • Israel (“God fights”): God first had to fight *with* him, but now God would fight *for* him. Jacob’s name was thus full of promise for Jacob and his descendants. • you have fought with God and with men: Through his entire life, Jacob had been seizing God’s blessing by his own abilities and by any means possible. Jacob knew the importance of the blessing, but he was too self\-sufficient and proud to let the blessing be given to him. He had been fighting God long before this encounter. • and have won: He had prevailed in his struggles with Esau and with Laban; now he prevailed in obtaining God’s blessing.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:28
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 25:26 (ID: `12517@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 32:29 (id: 12661)

Jacob knew who was with him ([32:30](https://ref.ly/Gen32:30)); the request was his attempt to regain some control. God would not reveal his name, which cannot be had on demand.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 32:30 (id: 12664)

Peniel (which means “face of God”): The name shows that Jacob recognized the man as a manifestation of God (a *theophany*). • yet my life has been spared (or *and I have been rescued*): The saying probably meant that Jacob realized that his prayer to be rescued from Esau ([32:11](https://ref.ly/Gen32:11)) had been answered, for if he could meet God like this and walk away, he had nothing to fear from Esau. The saying may also reflect an ancient understanding that no one could see God and live (see [Exod 33:20](https://ref.ly/Exod33:20)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Penuel (ID: `place:Penuel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 32:31 (id: 12665)

God injured Jacob’s hip, thus curtailing his proud self\-sufficiency. Since the Lord had restricted his natural strength, Jacob would have to rely on the Lord with greater faith. He had thought that returning to his land would be a matter of outwitting his brother once again ([32:3–21](https://ref.ly/Gen32:3-Gen32:21)), but here at the land’s threshold he met its true proprietor. He would get the land, but only if God fought for him. Self\-sufficiency—trying to achieve the blessing by our own strength or by the ways of the world—will not suffice. If we persist, God may have to cripple our self\-sufficiency to make us trust him more.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Penuel (ID: `place:Penuel`)

The story includes a dietary restriction for Israel that became a custom but was not put into law. This custom helped preserve the memory of the story. Observant Jews still refuse to eat the tendons of an animal’s hindquarters.

## Genesis 33:1–2 (id: 12670)

Jacob’s identity had been changed ([32:27–28](https://ref.ly/Gen32:27-Gen32:28)), but he had not yet learned to live up to the new name; he still showed the favoritism that divides families. He lined up his family and his possessions in the order of their importance to him, with the slave wives and their children in front (to face danger first), Leah’s group behind them, and Rachel and Joseph in the back, where it was safest.

## Genesis 33:1–17 (id: 12669)

Jacob’s long\-anticipated meeting with his brother Esau turned out far better than he had feared. Esau’s changed heart is an example of how “God fights” (See [32:28](https://ref.ly/Gen32:28)). Earlier, he had cared little about the birthright ([25:32–34](https://ref.ly/Gen25:32-Gen25:34)); now he cared little for old grudges. Jacob recognized that God had intervened.

## Genesis 34:1–31 (id: 12682)

Once Jacob and his family settled in the land, the Canaanite presence became a threat. This account is a stern warning to the Israelites about the possibility of their being defiled by the Canaanites. The nation of Israel was later commanded not to intermarry or make treaties with them, for they were a corrupt and corrupting people. This chapter implicitly warns against becoming familiar with the way they lived ([34:1–2](https://ref.ly/Gen34:1-Gen34:2)). It also taught Israel that in dealing with the Canaanites, they were to keep their integrity and not use the holy things of the covenant for deception and slaughter ([34:13](https://ref.ly/Gen34:13)); Israel’s reputation was at stake in the land ([34:30](https://ref.ly/Gen34:30)). For their ruthless violence, Simeon and Levi were passed over in the birthright blessing ([49:5–7](https://ref.ly/Gen49:5-Gen49:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:1-31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Dinah (ID: `person:Dinah`); Circumcision (ID: `keyterm:Circumcision`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Levi (ID: `person:Levi.3`); Simeon (ID: `person:Simeon.5`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Hivites (ID: `group:Hivites`); Bring Honor and Glory on Oneself (ID: `keyterm:BringHonorAndGloryOnOneself`); Foreskin (ID: `keyterm:Foreskin`); Perizzites (ID: `group:Perizzites`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Foolish (ID: `keyterm:Foolish`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Safety (ID: `keyterm:Safety`); Canaanites (ID: `group:Canaan`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Genesis 34:3–4 (id: 12686)

While Shechem’s intense feelings for Dinah made him willing to undergo significant hardship ([34:11–12](https://ref.ly/Gen34:11-Gen34:12), [17–18](https://ref.ly/Gen34:17-Gen34:18), [24](https://ref.ly/Gen34:24)), the way he showed his “love” for her was selfish, impetuous, and in violation of customary decency (cp. [Judg 14:2](https://ref.ly/Judg14:2); see [1 Cor 13:4–7](https://ref.ly/1Cor13:4-1Cor13:7); [1 Jn 4:10](https://ref.ly/1John4:10)). His character illustrated why Israel needed to remain separate from the Canaanites.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:3-4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Dinah (ID: `person:Dinah`)

## Genesis 34:5–7 (id: 12688)

Jacob’s response to this crime is surprising. When he heard that Dinah had been defiled, he said nothing about it until his sons came home. Because Jacob did not act, his sons did, though without the wisdom and integrity necessary for justice. Dinah’s full brothers were shocked and furious that Shechem had done such a disgraceful thing to Jacob’s family (literally *in Israel;* this is the first use of the name *Israel* to describe the family). This sexual crime was an outrage against the community of God’s people and deserved punishment, but the leader of the clan did nothing.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:5-7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Foolish (ID: `keyterm:Foolish`); Dinah (ID: `person:Dinah`)

## Genesis 34:8–10 (id: 12689)

Hamor proposed an alliance of intermarriage with the prospect of mutual economic benefit, but God had already promised Jacob everything, including the land ([34:10](https://ref.ly/Gen34:10); see [14:21–24](https://ref.ly/Gen14:21-Gen14:24)). Hamor wanted to gain control of Israel’s wealth ([34:23](https://ref.ly/Gen34:23)); no good could come of trusting the defiling Canaanites. For some of these reasons, intermarriage with Canaanites was not allowed under the law (see [Exod 23:27–33](https://ref.ly/Exod23:27-Exod23:33); [Deut 7:1–5](https://ref.ly/Deut7:1-Deut7:5))—unless, of course, they came to faith (see [Josh 2:1–15](https://ref.ly/Josh2:1-Josh2:15); [6:23–25](https://ref.ly/Josh6:23-Josh6:25); [Matt 1:5](https://ref.ly/Matt1:5)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:8-10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`)

## Genesis 34:13–17 (id: 12683)

Dinah’s brothers responded to the proposal without waiting for Jacob to respond, and they acted deceitfully. They may not have thought that Shechem and his people would ever agree to the rite of circumcision, but they knew what they would do if the Canaanites accepted ([34:25](https://ref.ly/Gen34:25)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:13-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Circumcision (ID: `keyterm:Circumcision`); Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Foreskin (ID: `keyterm:Foreskin`); Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Dinah (ID: `person:Dinah`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`)

## Genesis 34:18–24 (id: 12684)

The Canaanites accepted the stipulation and every male in the town was circumcised. This was not just to allow Shechem to marry Dinah, but to give them the opportunity to acquire everything that Jacob possessed ([34:23](https://ref.ly/Gen34:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:18-24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`); Circumcision (ID: `keyterm:Circumcision`); Bring Honor and Glory on Oneself (ID: `keyterm:BringHonorAndGloryOnOneself`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 34:25–29 (id: 12685)

When Simeon and Levi used circumcision to deceive and slaughter the Canaanites, they showed disdain for the sign of the covenant. Their slaughter of all the males and their plunder of the city was not justice, but brutal and excessive revenge. In their moral outrage and desire to right the wrong, they should have demanded compensation (see [Exod 22:16–17](https://ref.ly/Exod22:16-Exod22:17); [Deut 22:28–29](https://ref.ly/Deut22:28-Deut22:29)). Instead, their passionate act of rage cost them their birthright blessing ([Gen 49:5–7](https://ref.ly/Gen49:5-Gen49:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:25-29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Dinah (ID: `person:Dinah`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`); Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Simeon (ID: `person:Simeon.5`); Safety (ID: `keyterm:Safety`); Levi (ID: `person:Levi.3`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Genesis 35:1–29 (id: 12690)

This chapter highlights God’s promises, Jacob’s vow, and the transition to Jacob’s sons’ carrying on the covenant. Deborah, Rachel, and Isaac all died, marking the end of an era and of the account of Isaac’s family ([25:19–35:29](https://ref.ly/Gen25:19-Gen35:29)). • Idols were removed ([35:1–4](https://ref.ly/Gen35:1-Gen35:4)) and pure worship was established ([35:6–7](https://ref.ly/Gen35:6-Gen35:7)). During this transition, the faith had to be revitalized so that the covenant could be carried forward by Jacob’s sons.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 35:1-29

## Genesis 35:2–4 (id: 12697)

Jacob had vowed wholehearted devotion to the Lord ([28:20–22](https://ref.ly/Gen28:20-Gen28:22)); establishing this required that his family remove all pagan idols and cease their devotion to other gods. God permits no rivals; only the Lord was to be their God (cp. [Josh 5:1–9](https://ref.ly/Josh5:1-Josh5:9)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 35:2-4

## Genesis 35:11–12 (id: 12692)

God’s reference to himself as God Almighty assured Jacob that his promise could and would be fulfilled. At Bethel, God had promised that Jacob would have descendants in the land (cp. [28:13–14](https://ref.ly/Gen28:13-Gen28:14)); here he added that his descendants would include kings (see [17:6](https://ref.ly/Gen17:6)).

Benjamin's birth completed the family. But it was a sad event because Rachel died during childbirth (see study note on 31:32).

## Genesis 35:18 (id: 12695)

Rachel felt that the name Ben\-oni, meaning “son of my sorrow,” fit the moment. But Jacob did not want his son to carry such a sad name, so he changed it to Benjamin, meaning “son of my right hand.” In this way, Jacob changed a moment of sorrow into one of hope and gave his son a name that pointed to blessing and strength.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 35:18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Benjamin (ID: `person:Benjamin`)

## Genesis 35:19 (id: 12696)

Jacob did not carry Rachel’s body to the family tomb at Machpelah ([23:1–20](https://ref.ly/Gen23:1-Gen23:20); [25:9](https://ref.ly/Gen25:9); [49:30](https://ref.ly/Gen49:30); [50:13](https://ref.ly/Gen50:13)) but buried her in the territory that would be Benjamin’s (see [Josh 18:21–28](https://ref.ly/Josh18:21-Josh18:28); [1 Sam 10:2](https://ref.ly/1Sam10:2)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 35:19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bethlehem (ID: `place:Bethlehem`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`)

## Genesis 35:20 (id: 12698)

*it marks Rachel’s tomb to this day**:* This seems to be a later editorial comment by someone who was living in the land after the conquest and giving directions to the tomb (see Genesis Book Introduction, “Composition”).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 35:20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 35:22 (id: 12699)

Reuben had intercourse with Bilhah, thus defiling his father’s marriage bed. Perhaps Reuben, as the oldest son, was trying to replace his father as head of the clan by a pagan procedure (cp. [2 Sam 16:15–22](https://ref.ly/2Sam16:15-2Sam16:22)), but by this action he lost his birthright (see [Gen 49:3–4](https://ref.ly/Gen49:3-Gen49:4)). • Jacob soon heard about it, but he again delayed his response (see [49:3–4](https://ref.ly/Gen49:3-Gen49:4); cp. [34:5](https://ref.ly/Gen34:5)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 35:22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Reuben (ID: `person:Reuben`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Bilhah (ID: `person:Bilhah`)

## Genesis 36:1–8 (id: 12707)

The account of Esau stresses two points. First, Esau’s sons were born . . . in the land of Canaan ([36:5](https://ref.ly/Gen36:5)) before he moved to Seir ([36:8](https://ref.ly/Gen36:8)). Jacob’s children, by contrast, were almost all born outside the land but then moved into it. God was giving the land to Jacob and his descendants and so made room for them by providing for Esau in a different place. Second, Esau’s other name was Edom. Israel often struggled with the Edomites (see [1 Sam 21:7](https://ref.ly/1Sam21:7); [22:9–22](https://ref.ly/1Sam22:9-1Sam22:22); [Obad 1](https://ref.ly/Obad1:1-Obad1:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 36:1-8

Esau’s three wives—Adah, Oholibamah, and Basemath—are listed. Two of these wives’ names are different from those listed earlier ([26:34](https://ref.ly/Gen26:34); [28:9](https://ref.ly/Gen28:9)). ## Genesis 37:1 (id: 12723)

In chapter [36](https://ref.ly/Gen36:1-Gen36:43), Esau was increasing in power and wealth. In contrast, Jacob, still waiting for the promise, lived in the land as a foreigner, like his father. He remained a temporary resident with a single family. 

Worldly greatness often comes more quickly than spiritual greatness. Waiting for the promised spiritual blessing while others prosper tests patience, faith, and perseverance.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 37:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 37:2 (id: 12731)

The account of Jacob and his family tells of Joseph and his brothers. Jacob is still prominent, but the focus is on Joseph, who is introduced as an obedient seventeen\-year\-old son. • Joseph reported . . . the bad things his brothers were doing: Bringing a bad report has never been popular, but it was the right thing to do and shows that Joseph was faithful from the beginning. As the story progresses, we see more of his brothers’ wickedness displayed until, like Cain, they tried to eliminate the brother who pleased God.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 37:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Bilhah (ID: `person:Bilhah`); Zilpah (ID: `person:Zilpah`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Genesis 37:2–50.26 (id: 12724)

The story of Joseph and his brothers comprises a separate unit in Genesis, distinct in tone and emphasis from the preceding material. It traces one continuous series of episodes with Joseph at their center. • Cycles of repeated motifs structure the entire Joseph account. The themes are closely related to those found in wisdom books such as Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, and Daniel. Wisdom literature assures the faithful that God brings good out of evil and joy out of pain—if not in this life, then certainly in the life to come. Though the wicked may prosper for a time, the righteous should hold fast to their integrity because a higher, more enduring principle of life is realized through obedience to God. Everyone who aspires to leadership in God’s plan should observe how wisdom led to Joseph’s success. Christ Jesus embodied the life of wisdom portrayed here as no one else could, for he is the wisdom of God. • Judah is also an important character in the story. He began as irresponsible and mean\-spirited as his brothers; but he truly repented, put his life on the line to ransom a child for his father, and received a very important inheritance.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 37:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.4`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Benjamin (ID: `person:Benjamin`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Ephraim (ID: `person:Ephraim`); Goshen (ID: `place:Goshen`); Manasseh (ID: `person:Manasseh`); Reuben (ID: `person:Reuben`); Peace (ID: `keyterm:IntactState`); Simeon (ID: `person:Simeon.5`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Appoint (ID: `keyterm:Appoint`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Shelah (ID: `person:Shelah`); Hebrews (ID: `group:Hebrews`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Nile River (ID: `place:Nile`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`)

Jacob: Hebrew *Israel;* also in [37:13](https://ref.ly/Gen37:13). • Jacob loved Joseph more because Joseph had been born to him in his old age and because he was the first son of his favorite wife, Rachel. • Jacob gave Joseph a beautiful robe to demonstrate that he intended to grant him the largest portion of the inheritance.

## Genesis 37:4 (id: 12738)

Jacob’s favoritism toward Joseph inflamed his other sons’ hatred of their brother. Just as Isaac’s and Rebekah’s favoritism had separated their family, Jacob’s favoritism would separate him from his son Joseph.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 37:4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Peace (ID: `keyterm:IntactState`)

## Genesis 37:5–11 (id: 12739)

God confirmed his choice of this faithful son as the eventual leader of the whole family through two symbolic dreams. This is the first of three dream sequences in chs [37–50](https://ref.ly/Gen37:1-Gen50:26) (see [40:1–41:36](https://ref.ly/Gen40:1-Gen41:36); cp. [15:13](https://ref.ly/Gen15:13); [20:3](https://ref.ly/Gen20:3); [28:12–15](https://ref.ly/Gen28:12-Gen28:15); [31:24](https://ref.ly/Gen31:24)). Dreams carried weight as a form of divine communication, especially if the dream revelation was given twice.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 37:5-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be Zealous (ID: `keyterm:Zealous`); Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); Sheaf (ID: `realia:Sheaf`)

## Genesis 37:7 (id: 12740)

The *sheaves of grain* suggest how Joseph would gain authority over his family (see [42:1–3](https://ref.ly/Gen42:1-Gen42:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 37:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sheaf (ID: `realia:Sheaf`)

## Genesis 37:8 (id: 12741)

The brothers reacted angrily to the revelation, unlike Joseph, who was honest and faithful. This shows why they were not chosen as leaders. Leaders in God's plan cannot be full of jealousy and hatred. 

In their anger, they missed an important part of the revelation. They also would become rulers ("stars," [37:9](https://ref.ly/Gen37:9)) and would successfully gather their sheaves ([37:7](https://ref.ly/Gen37:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 37:8


## Genesis 37:9 (id: 12742)

*the sun and moon and eleven stars:* Signs like the sun, moon, and stars often stand for rulers. The dream showed that Joseph would become a leader over the whole family of Israel.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 37:9


## Genesis 37:10 (id: 12718)

*your mother … and I:* Joseph's birth mother, Rachel, was dead ([35:19](https://ref.ly/Gen35:19)). Leah was now the matriarch of the clan.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 37:10


## Genesis 37:11 (id: 12719)

His brothers hated Joseph because they were jealous of him. Rather than recognize the hand of God, the brothers tried to prevent the dream from being fulfilled ([37:18–36](https://ref.ly/Gen37:18-Gen37:36)). These actions show that they were not fit to lead the household of faith. God’s sovereign choice of a leader, especially if the one chosen is young or appears unqualified, often brings out the true colors of those who have to submit to that leader’s authority. The brothers represent people throughout history who have been driven by envy and malice because they were not committed to doing the Lord’s will. • Jacob wondered what the dreams meant because he knew that God would choose the next leader, that God could choose the younger son to rule over the older sons, and that God could reveal all this in dreams.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 37:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be Zealous (ID: `keyterm:Zealous`)

## Genesis 37:12–13 (id: 12720)

It was foolish for Jacob to send Joseph on such a mission, knowing how the brothers felt about him. Joseph obeyed his father by going to find his brothers despite their hatred for him.

## Genesis 37:28 (id: 12729)

the Ishmaelites, who were Midianite traders (literally *the Midianite traders*): Ishmaelites were descendants of Abraham through Hagar ([16:5](https://ref.ly/Gen16:5)), while Midianites were descendants of Abraham through Keturah ([25:1–2](https://ref.ly/Gen25:1-Gen25:2)). The term *Ishmaelite* may have described bedouin tribes generally. The Midianites might also have been traveling with a separate caravan of *Ishmaelite traders* ([37:27](https://ref.ly/Gen37:27)). • Kidnapping (see [40:15](https://ref.ly/Gen40:15)) would become a capital offense (see [Exod 21:16](https://ref.ly/Exod21:16)) in Jewish law.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 37:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ishmaelites (ID: `group:Ishmael`); Midian (ID: `place:Midian`); Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); Ishmael (ID: `person:Ishmael`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Midianites (ID: `group:Midian`); Pit (ID: `realia:Pit`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Genesis 38:1–30 (id: 12743)

The story of Judah and Tamar is a carefully placed break in the larger story. It shows what was happening in Judah’s family. Judah would later become an important leader. The story also shows the early stages of the family mixing with the people of the land. This helps explain why God sent the family to Egypt (chapters [39–47](https://ref.ly/Gen39:1-Gen47:31)). 

The Egyptians kept themselves separate from other peoples ([43:32](https://ref.ly/Gen43:32)). Because of that, the Israelites were more likely to keep their own identity in Egypt than in Canaan.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 38:1-30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tamar (ID: `person:Tamar`); Shelah (ID: `person:Shelah`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Adullamites (ID: `group:Adullam`); Timnah (ID: `place:Timnah`); Er (ID: `person:Er.2`); Onan (ID: `person:Onan`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Adullam (ID: `place:Adullam`); Enaim (ID: `place:Enaim`); Shua (ID: `person:Shua`); Hirah (ID: `person:Hirah`); Kid (ID: `fauna:Kid`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Thread (ID: `realia:Thread`); Zerah (ID: `person:Zerah.3`); Perez (ID: `person:Perez`); Measuring Reed (ID: `realia:MeasuringReed`); Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`); Play the Prostitute (ID: `keyterm:PlayTheProstitute`); Achzib (ID: `place:Achzib`); Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Canaanites (ID: `group:Canaan`)

## Genesis 38:8 (id: 12756)

as our law requires. . . . You must produce an heir for your brother: The custom that informs this episode is the law for levirate marriage (Latin levir, “husband’s brother”). By this custom, which was later incorporated into God’s law for Israel ([Deut 25:5–10](https://ref.ly/Deut25:5-Deut25:10)), if a man died childless, his brother or nearest relative would marry his widow to produce a child who would carry on the family name of the deceased and inherit his property. Apparently, the near kinsman had a right to refuse, but he would be disgraced in the family for refusing to perpetuate his brother’s name.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 38:8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Onan (ID: `person:Onan`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`)

## Genesis 38:9–10 (id: 12757)

Onan married Tamar, but was not willing to provide an heir for his brother. He would have sex with Tamar but not fulfill his responsibility to his dead brother ([38:8](https://ref.ly/Gen38:8)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 38:9-10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Onan (ID: `person:Onan`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 38:11 (id: 12744)

Judah is now presented as the model for his sons’ behavior—he, too, was unfaithful to his levirate responsibility to Tamar as next kinsman (see study note on 38:8). Judah and his sons were far too Canaanite in their ways (see study note on 38:27–30; contrast Boaz, [Ruth 3–4](https://ref.ly/Ruth3:1-Ruth4:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 38:11

## Genesis 38:14–19 (id: 12746)

Tamar realized that she would have to take matters into her own hands if the family were to have a future. Tamar acted in keeping with the levirate custom (see study note on 38:8) out of loyalty to her deceased husband. She had a legal right to an heir by Judah’s son or by Judah, so she lured her father\-in\-law into having sex with her. Jacob’s family was deceived again, this time by a Canaanite daughter\-in\-law.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 38:14-19
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 38:8 (ID: `12756@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

## Genesis 38:26 (id: 12751)

She is more righteous than I am: Judah acknowledged that he had shirked his responsibility to provide an heir. It was sinful for Judah to go to a prostitute, but Tamar had a legal right to be the mother of Judah’s child and had acted on that right. In the book of Ruth, the elders analogously blessed the marriage of Boaz and Ruth, praying that God would make Ruth like Tamar ([Ruth 4:12](https://ref.ly/Ruth4:12); cp. [Matt 1:3](https://ref.ly/Matt1:3), [5](https://ref.ly/Matt1:5)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 38:26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Shelah (ID: `person:Shelah`); Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`)

## Genesis 38:27–30 (id: 12752)

Judah’s line continued because of Tamar. The twins replaced Judah’s two slain sons ([38:7](https://ref.ly/Gen38:7), [10](https://ref.ly/Gen38:10)); their birth was similar to the birth of Jacob and Esau ([25:21–26](https://ref.ly/Gen25:21-Gen25:26)) in that the “red” one was born first, but the other son pushed past him in later life. Jacob’s gaining the right to rule over his older brother ([27:29](https://ref.ly/Gen27:29)) seemed to be relived in Judah’s line. The line was carried on through Perez and not through the elder son Shelah, whom he had gone to such lengths to protect ([38:11](https://ref.ly/Gen38:11); see [1 Chr 4:21](https://ref.ly/1Chr4:21)), nor through the elder twin Zerah (see [Ruth 4:13–22](https://ref.ly/Ruth4:13-Ruth4:22); [Matt 1:3](https://ref.ly/Matt1:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 38:27-30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Perez (ID: `person:Perez`); Zerah (ID: `person:Zerah.3`)

## Genesis 39:1–47.31 (id: 12759)

Joseph began as a slave, alienated from his brothers and separated from his father; he ended as Pharaoh’s viceroy. Through the trips to Egypt, the covenant family went from the brink of apostasy, divided by jealousy and deception, to being reconciled and united by Judah’s intercession and Joseph’s forgiveness.

## Genesis 39:6–10 (id: 12765)

One of the major motifs of wisdom literature (see study note on 37:2–50:26) is to warn young people that immorality will lead them to disaster (cp. [Prov 5–7](https://ref.ly/Prov5:1-Prov7:24)). Joseph was able to resist temptation because he had godly wisdom—he was guided by the fear of the Lord ([Prov 1:7](https://ref.ly/Prov1:7); [9:10](https://ref.ly/Prov9:10)).
